Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Article: Do Bond Investors Get Rewarded for Taking Dur. Risk? (P. 1) Edward F. McQuarrie, William J. Bernstein, 11/6

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Sure, I get the 'look at longest data' thing, but I am not quite sure that pre-Volcker efforts to promote employment by lowering rates even if it resulted in inflation is relevant after finding that employment is actually better fostered by having stable inflation because it allows companies to plan better.

Anyway, given their statement that:

As an aside: The examples thus far show why you need to use the longest available period of data when examining historical bond returns, and not short, cherry-picked time spans. Be wary of those who ignore that imperative.

I wonder why they also say:

To avoid the complications posed by the deflation of the 1930s and the yield curve
control exercised by the Fed during the war years, we show only the post-1945 data.

I mean if we can assume that deflation won't happen again and we can assume that the Fed won't use yield curve control again, they why can't we assume the pre-Volcker policies won't happen again?

It just seems logically inconsistent but yeah, they favor some assumptions but not others. Ok, but surely it colors the results.

Lazio, gol di testa e difesa solida: resta il nodo degli avvii di ripresa

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Sei reti arrivano di testa, come per la Roma; tre dei nove gol subiti sono invece maturati subito dopo l’intervallo.

La Lazio ha segnato 25 gol e ne ha subiti 9 nelle prime 11 giornate. Secondo quanto riportato da Il Messaggero, La squadra di Gattuso ha raggiunto 29 punti, superando il precedente primato biancoceleste di 28 nel 2017/18. Al rendimento offensivo e alla tenuta difensiva si accompagna però un dato da correggere: tre delle reti incassate sono arrivate nei primi cinque minuti della ripresa.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

I biancocelesti continuano a segnare nonostante le pressioni elevate e le assenze pesanti. Secondo Il Messaggero, per ritrovare una Lazio così incisiva nei primi 11 turni bisogna risalire al 1936/37, quando la squadra era guidata da Jozsef Viola.

Noslin e compagni trovano la rete attraverso azioni costruite, ripartenze e conclusioni dalla distanza. Sono arrivati anche sei gol di testa: tanti quanti quelli della Roma, un primato europeo secondo i dati riportati. Il totale delle reti è già a quota 25; la Lazio allenata da Sarri raggiunse quota 26 soltanto a inizio marzo.

Nei pareggi contro Milan e Napoli, i biancocelesti avrebbero potuto chiudere la partita già nel primo tempo. I dati sulle conclusioni descrivono però una squadra particolarmente efficace rispetto alle occasioni create: è decima in Serie A per tiri complessivi e nona per tiri nello specchio, mentre i 25 gol segnati sono otto in più dei 17 previsti dagli xG.

Pur senza un vero stoccatore, la Lazio beneficia degli inserimenti di Frattesi. Gattuso lo ha definito «uno dei migliori invasori d’Europa», sottolineando una qualità che contribuisce alla capacità realizzativa della squadra.

Un mese fa Gattuso ridimensionava il peso della gestione del pallone: «non mi interessa nulla, dobbiamo andare sempre in avanti». La crescita della sua Lazio passa, tuttavia, anche dal palleggio. A fine settembre la squadra era quindicesima nel campionato per possesso; dopo le percentuali registrate a ottobre è salita al sesto posto.

Quando affiora la stanchezza, i biancocelesti ricorrono alla manovra per mantenere il controllo del gioco. È successo anche dopo l’accelerazione del Napoli al rientro dall’intervallo: il tecnico ha riconosciuto la capacità dei suoi di «riequilibrare la partita» tenendo il pallone e allentando la pressione avversaria.

WEBTOON: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On its third quarter of 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 9, 2026, WEBTOON (WBTN +2.69%) announced projected fourth-quarter revenue growth of 9% to 12% on a constant currency basis, an operational metric that excludes foreign currency fluctuations. Long-term investors should care because accelerating advertising sales and expanding artificial intelligence tools show the core business is regaining momentum despite revenue contraction in Japan. Three key takeaways from management stand out.

Advertising provides a higher-margin revenue stream than paid digital comic chapters. Scaling ad sales in North America and Korea lifts gross margin while reducing dependence on platform transaction fees.

Japan represents the world's largest digital comic market, yet app household penetration sits below 20%. Management is committing 10 billion Japanese yen, or roughly $63 million in U.S. dollars, over three years to fund local creators and revive paid user growth.

Automatic translation breaks down geographic barriers for stories on CANVAS, the self-publishing open platform. Broader language access increases episode reads, driving global readers into paid content funnels without adding manual translation overhead.

Fourth-quarter momentum reflects organic platform expansion, led by Korean paid content and global ad formats. Management is watching web-to-app user conversions while deploying capital into intellectual property adaptations.

Technology innovation continues to reshape media distribution. Owning original comic stories positions the business to capture downstream licensing value across global entertainment formats.

Google AI spots high blood pressure using ordinary watch sensors

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Google's smartwatch AI detected 65.8% of hypertension cases in a study, pointing to cuff-free screening using everyday sensor data.

Google researchers and medical collaborators have used ordinary smartwatch signals to flag possible high blood pressure without calibrating the watch against an arm cuff.

A study published in Nature on Nov. 5 suggests a way to prompt clinical checks during everyday wear, although the system missed about 33% of hypertension cases.

The AI identified 65.8% of participants with hypertension according to a clinical reference test (sensitivity), while it correctly screened 90% of those without the condition as negative (specificity).

The prospective, multicenter study followed 196 participants through seven days of smartwatch monitoring and compared results with 24-hour ambulatory blood-pressure monitoring. Participants had no known cardiovascular disease and were not taking blood-pressure medication, limiting how far the findings extend to people already receiving treatment.

The 95% confidence intervals were 54% to 76.3% for sensitivity and 83.2% to 94.7% for specificity.

The research system turns seven days of pulse and motion data into a screening score. Its detection rate leaves a substantial blind spot.

Of participants with hypertension on 24-hour ambulatory monitoring, 65.8% were detected and 34.2% were missed.

Of participants without hypertension on the reference test, 90.0% screened negative and 10.0% produced false alerts.

Screening can flag a reason for a clinical check; a negative result cannot exclude hypertension.

196 participants in a selected, risk-enriched cohort, without known cardiovascular disease or antihypertensive medication. Bars show conditional study rates, not predictive values for all smartwatch users. This is screening research, not a standalone diagnosis.

Reported sensitivity 65.8% and specificity 90.0% from the 196-person prospective study. JavaScript arithmetic: 100-65.8=34.2% missed; 100-90.0=10.0% false alerts. Bar lengths are proportional to these shares; no hypothetical patient counts. 95% confidence intervals retain reported precision.

196 participants in a selected, risk-enriched cohort, without known cardiovascular disease or antihypertensive medication. Bars show conditional study rates, not predictive values for all smartwatch users. This is screening research, not a standalone diagnosis.

The system, called AI-PPG-ACC-HTN, combines optical pulse-wave measurements with accelerometer signals.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

According to Google Research's explanation, it aggregates those signals across days and combines them with the user's height to produce a probability of hypertension.

It does not report an exact systolic and diastolic blood-pressure reading. No personal cuff reading is needed to calibrate the smartwatch system, though cuff-based measurements remained the reference the researchers used to test it.

The waveform model was pretrained using 492,347 hours of unlabeled sensor data from 2,109 consenting participants.

The study supplement describes the prospective cohort as intentionally risk-enriched. In this selected group, 80.6% of positive smartwatch screens matched hypertension on the reference test, the measure known as positive predictive value.

The research involved Google Research, Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center and Harvard Medical School.

The authors describe a possible role for smartwatch screening in prompting formal assessment and confirmatory diagnosis using established cuff-based methods, with the watch prompting a clinical check.

Manchester United are reportedly considering a move for Mauricio Pochettino as the club prepares for a potential managerial change at Old Trafford.

The former Tottenham Hotspur and Chelsea manager is understood to be open to taking charge of the Red Devils, as Michael Carrick faces an increasingly uncertain future after a disappointing start to the Premier League campaign.

According to a report from TEAMtalk, Pochettino would welcome the opportunity to manage Manchester United, although the club have not made direct contact with the Argentine or his representatives.

The 54-year-old is currently managing the United States national team, having taken charge in 2024. He recently signed a contract extension that keeps him in the role until 2030, although an exit clause could make a return to European club football possible.

Pochettino has considerable Premier League experience, having previously managed Tottenham and Chelsea. His impressive work at Spurs, where he guided the London club to the Champions League final in 2019, could make him an attractive appointment for United.

He also came close to taking the Old Trafford job in 2022 before the club eventually appointed Erik ten Hag.

Meanwhile, Carrick’s position has become increasingly difficult following a disappointing run of results. United have managed just one victory in their last ten league matches, leaving them in the relegation zone.

The club’s hierarchy, including director of football Jason Wilcox and chief executive Omar Berrada, are expected to discuss the manager’s future with co-owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe.

Several alternatives have also been considered, including former Newcastle United manager Eddie Howe, Julian Nagelsmann, Antonio Conte and Unai Emery.

However, Pochettino’s willingness to consider the opportunity could strengthen his chances of becoming Manchester United’s next manager.

It remains to be seen whether United will formally approach the Argentine, but a managerial change during the international break could open the door for an appointment at Old Trafford.

PitchBook opens its platform to startups for free and debuts Origin deal sourcing service

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Private markets data provider PitchBook Data Inc. today opened its platform to startups for free at Web Summit in Lisbon.

Those that sign up have to share their financials. Some of that information will reach investors through the company’s new deal sourcing service, PitchBook Origin.

The launches come as more startups than ever are landing a first round. In the U.S., the 2021 record for first-time financings had already fallen by the end of September, PitchBook and the National Venture Capital Association found in their latest Venture Monitor report.

PitchBook credits artificial intelligence with part of that growth, since the technology has made companies easier to start. AI tools have also compressed investor diligence from weeks to days. Sourcing has not kept pace, the company argues, particularly where the signals are not public.

Free access for startups runs through a program called PitchBook for Startups. Founders who have bootstrapped or raised only from angels, grants or accelerators qualify as long as they are now seeking a first institutional round and can show at least a mockup of their product.

Once a startup has handed over its financials and details on its leadership team and product, its founders can use the PitchBook platform to research which firms are actively writing checks in their sector and stage. Investor research tops PitchBook’s own list of what founders can do with the access.

That founder data lands in a much bigger pool on the Origin side, where coverage starts at about 25 million companies and 250 million people profiles. Bootstrapped businesses and companies that have not yet raised money or announced what they are building are part of that count, and some 15 million of the companies are new to PitchBook.

Third-party data enrichment and PitchBook’s own machine learning models supply part of the coverage. PitchBook said it has not had founder-verified data at scale before.

Each company gets a score, and investors can tune relevance filters to their own criteria. Real-time alerts, saved searches and customer relationship management integrations are meant to replace the manual monitoring that deal teams do now.

Chief Solutions Officer Paul Santarelli said the missing piece for investors has been visibility into “the stage before a company raises, when the most important signals are the hardest to see.” Every company that joins the startup program and contributes verified information sharpens that picture, he said.

PitchBook “was once a startup itself, working to get in front of the right backers,” President and Chief Operating Officer Rod Diefendorf said. Founded in 2007, the Seattle company now serves more than 100,000 clients worldwide. Morningstar Inc. has owned it since 2016.

Startups can apply for the program now. Origin is available as a standalone product or as an add-on to the main PitchBook platform.

Opinion: Liverpool have found their next Mo Salah, he's going to be incredible

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Liverpool have found their next Mo Salah, and he's going to be incredible.

Nobody wanted things to come to an end this summer. Things had gone badly for Salah in his final season at Anfield.

The conflict between him and Arne Slot ultimately forced him to make a decision he might no have made otherwise.

With the benefit of hindsight. Knowing both Slot and Richard Hughes as well as Michael Edwards left the club, Salah might have opted to stay put.

However, he made the decision to leave and agreed with Liverpool to rip up the final year on his contract in return for a new adventure.

The Egyptian opted to find that adventure in Turkey, where he was welcomed like a hero in Trabzonspor and the rest is history.

Liverpool are missing Mo SalahUltimately, the Reds then spent the summer trying to replace Salah but to no avail. The club's main target, Yan Diomande, opted to favour Paris Saint Germain's advances over the Reds and then he moved to Real Madrid. Yankuba Minteh and Ismaila Sarr were targeted as right-sided wingers but Liverpool never pulled the trigger on those moves deeming them too expensive. It means the Reds now have four natural left-wingers and no recognised right-winger in the squad - except for 16-year-old Joshua Abe. At the same time, Salah is flourishing in Trabzonspor. The 34-year-old looks quick and he's beating defenders for fun. On top of that, he also has 16 goal contributions in 16 games for the club. Liverpool could do with someone of his quality right now.

Still unfit and still angry | West Ham fans won’t be at all surprised

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Leopards, it is said, do not change their spots. Neither, it would seem, do out-of-favour ‘wantaways’ who refuse to play for one manager having fallen out with his predecessors, despite being made a millionaire by the club that employs him, change their mindsets.

Such would seem to be the case with the talented yet angst-ridden package that is Jean Clair Todibo, once of Barcelona, now of West Ham yet loaned out through sheer frustration to Lens back in his native France.

Yet Todibo hasn’t managed to make the mindset leap towards peace, contentment, and a regular starting slot.

It’s all relayed by insidefutbol.com in their article

…Lens boss Yannick Cahuzac has played down West Ham United loanee Jean-Clair Todibo being angry about being substituted off against Lille and insists the defender’s fitness is being worked on.

On Friday, against Lille, the West Ham centre-back started the game and he was substituted in the 62nd minute mark, a decision which left the player visibly frustrated and angry. “

Todibo now on loan at French side Lens but still not pulling up any trees: Is anyone surprised?

Revealing that even now, two and a half years since Todibo’s arrival at West Ham, the player is still not fit for a full game. Surprise, surprise.

Did he ever manage 90 minutes in a West Ham shirt? I think maybe just once. Some players have ‘trouble’ all over them and Todibo, ever since his Barcelona exit, has struggled to find a place where he can hold down a squad place and keep his seemingly volatile nature in check.

Whether he will ever fully solve his ‘fitness issues’ after his dreadful childhood accident, who knows. His very existence near the top of the football pyramid is testament to his enormous mental strength. But more’s the pity that Tim Steidten didn’t choose to give him a very, very wide berth, instead choosing to whisk Todibo to West Ham on the private jet in 2024.

Another one in the ‘West Ham hall of fame crap transfers’: And he’s got another two years on his West Ham contract even after this season’s loan is over.

From the old Bobby Moore Upper to the Billy Bonds' stand these days - I've been watching since '03 and a supporter since about 1970.. Favourite player - Dean Ashton: Curse you Shaun Wright-Phillips. I still watch YouTube repeats of the Cup Final of 2006 hoping in vain that Shaka Hislop grows another six inches and stops Steven Gerrard's injury time equaliser. Can tell I'm getting old knowing I watched both Mark Noble's debut and his last game at West Ham. Pulling on a Claret and Blue replica shirt still makes me feel the same butterflies as when I was seven years old. Magic.

The Amazon Alexa Photo Frame uses AI to smartly curate your huge collection of pictures, and present them in an orderly fashion. However, it's best for those who have an Amazon Prime subscription.

Lower resolution display than comparably priced models

Amazon Prime or Alexa+ subscription needed

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

I never thought a gadget would make me want to cry — least of all one from Amazon.

After unboxing and setting up the Alexa Photo Frame, one of the first images it displayed was a black-and-white picture of my father, who has been deceased for 16 years, feeding a squirrel.

I remember having the photo stored digitally somewhere, but it’s been several years since I last saw it. But the Alexa Photo Frame pulled it up, and juxtaposed it next to another picture of a squirrel clinging to a screen door. The Photo Frame also wasted no time digging up scores of photos of our family cat, who also died around the same time as my dad.

It was a very emotional first few minutes with this device.

But the Alexa Photo Frame also went on to deliver photos of my child playing, vacations and other happy occasions, which brought back more pleasant emotions.

While I have plenty of criticisms of AI and its use, this is one instance where I feel like it delivers something positive, and makes this one of the best photo frames I’ve ever used.

This might be stating the obvious, but if you want to get the most out of the Alexa Photo Frame, you’re going to need either an Alexa+ ($19/month) or an Amazon Prime subscription ($149/year), so you can use Amazon’s AI to its fullest.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

The Alexa Photo Frame uses Amazon’s AI in one of the smartest ways yet. It combs through your entire library of images, and then pulls them up based on criteria such as the time of year, themes, objects, and events.

So, during the fall, you might be served pictures of pumpkins and leaves falling; in the summer, it might be photos of you at the beach.

While the Alexa Photo Frame only works in landscape mode, it does a clever thing with all the portrait photos in your library. If, for instance, you have photos of your child blowing out birthday candles over the years, it might put two of those images side by side, to show you the changes over the years. The photos of my dad and the squirrel was one example; another was two pictures of my kid potty training.

Or, if you want to go the more direct route, you can also ask Alexa to “show me photos of cats and kids,” and it will create a curated slideshow of all those images from your library.

Alexa+ isn’t perfect; when I asked it to create a slideshow of my daughter, a number of the pictures were of old ladies and some other random kids. However, it’s smart enough so that when one of these images showed up, I said “Alexa, that’s not my daughter,” and it went back through and edited the collection so those mismatched photos were no longer there.

Of course, the Photo Frame can act as a smart speaker. You definitely wouldn’t want to use it to play music, but you can use Alexa+ on it as you would any other Amazon smart speaker. And, should you not want it to listen, you can press the microphone mute button on the back.

Another trick with the Alexa Photo Frame is that you can attach short audio files to pictures, so if you’re gifting it to your parents, you can add a photo of your kids around a Christmas tree, with a voice note wishing them happy holidays. You have to tap the photo on the screen to hear the note, so it’s a nice extra, but I’m not sure how much it will be used.

Specs-wise, the Alexa Photo Frame isn’t the most impressive when compared to the best digital photo frames. The $229 Aura Aspen, for example, has a 12-inch screen with a much higher resolution of 1600 x 1200, and can be set up in portrait or landscape mode. The Lexar Pexar has an even crisper 2000 x 1200 display, and costs even less, at $179. And, the Alexa Photo Frame can’t play videos, which also make up a good portion of my phone’s library.

But neither makes curating all your photos as effortless — or as ingenious — as what Amazon does with Alexa+. For me, it’s solved the biggest issue I’ve had with digital photo frames and my massive photo library. How do I rediscover those old photos, and how do I make them show up on my photo frame without spending hours combing through everything?

'Pass to Steph!' - Butler issues wise words from bench as Warriors win again

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Steph Curry moves 16th on the NBA's all-time points-scorers list as he impresses in the Golden State Warriors' victory over the New Orleans Pelicans; the Warriors have won eight straight since 0-3 start to the season with latest victory perhaps down to Jimmy Butler's clipboard...

Injured guard Jimmy Butler dressed up as a member of the Golden State Warriors' coaching staff and held up a clipboard with the message 'Pass to Steph' as the team made it eight wins in a row in the NBA.

The Steph in question, the legendary Stephen Curry, scored 25 points as the Warriors beat the New Orleans Pelicans 138-87 - the largest win by any team so far this season.

Curry passed DeMar DeRozan (26,800) for 16th place on the NBA's all-time career scoring list, with the 38-year-old's tally now 26,803.

Warriors head coach Steve Kerr said of Butler's input: "I told [assistant coach] Khalid (Robinson] 'one of these days we should have Jimmy dress up like us'.

"He's always got some great outfit on, something fashionable, and we wear our quarter-zips and sweaters.

"I said: 'we should really get him to dress like a coach one day' and then he walks out and he's dressed like us.

"I turned to Khalid and said: 'did you tell him to do that?' He goes: 'no'.

"It was, I guess, mental telepathy or something. We're on the same page on that. He was enjoying himself."

On Butler's coaching credentials, Kerr added: "He said all the right things - how many team fouls we had. He was really acting the part."

Butler is currently recovering from surgery on his right knee back in February after tearing his anterior cruciate ligament.

Warriors youngster Yaxel Lendeborg said of his team-mate:" For me, especially, he's always just giving me little tips and tricks on the court.

Butler's message was simple on Monday night, though, added Lendeborg: "Just give the ball to Steph, that's the most serious thing he said."

The Warriors' eight-match winning streak comes after they began the season 0-3.

Bitcoin (BTC) Capitulation: Retail Holders Are Melting, Dropping BTC Off

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Bitcoin has been losing wallets on the network since October 16 of this year. With the number of non-empty wallets peaking at 59.99 million that day, the network has already lost roughly 108,700 of them as the price dropped back below $75,000. Smaller holders are clearly running out of patience.

Bitcoin's last bullish hope was a short recovery rally towards the $80,000–$81,000 price threshold in early November. It failed to hold on to that momentum and quickly tumbled towards the $76,000 area, which opened the way towards more losses. Since its October top near $87,000, BTC lost roughly 14% of its value and lost everything it built up since late August.

BTC moves below all major moving averages, with most of them aiming downwards and hinting at the aggravation of the current trend on the market. RSI confirms the thesis, moving around 40 and suggesting a bearish momentum, or a sideways movement at least.

Fueled by the falling price, retail is still fearful: smaller traders are merging or emptying their wallets. The network now holds around 59.88 million non-empty wallets, while exchange supply is down near 1.28 million BTC. The shrinking wallet count, however, isn't something that automatically means Bitcoin is being used less.

But where would BTC finally settle down? Realistically, the closest 'fair' support is the $74,000–$75,000 area, where buyers already stepped in this week. Discussing a breakdown below makes little sense, unfortunately, due to the volatile nature of the market.

But even if BTC holds the $74,000 support, bulls might still stay sidelined. There are little to no signs of retail coming back that can help BTC reach key market levels. Even worse, the market in general isn't favoring bulls, so bracing yourself for a prolonged correction might be the wise choice.

In case bulls somehow wake up, a recovery towards $78,000–$80,000 is the main priority, as it's the area where several moving averages are sitting right now. A falling wallet count with the price holding often acts as a sign of sellers getting exhausted, as smaller investors tend to give up when fear is at its highest.

Whether buyers can defend the $74,000 level or not is the main test for the asset right now. The wallet count keeps shrinking, the price is stuck below the moving averages and a short-lived price bounce here won't change the situation as a whole, unfortunately.

The former Brighton defender believes England fans would not boo him if they knew the full story of his exit from England’s 2022 World Cup squad.

But the 29-year-old insists he will not ‘throw anyone under the bus’ by talking about it.

White walked out of the camp in Qatar due to ‘personal reasons’, reported to be an alleged fallout with assistant manager Steve Holland, and subsequently made himself unavailable for the rest of Sir Gareth Southgate’s time in charge.

He returned to Thomas Tuchel’s squad in March, but was jeered by England supporters before being ruled out of this summer’s World Cup through injury.

The 29-year-old says he stands by his decision of four years ago, but is looking forward to his ‘story continuing’.

‘It’s something that I’ve not spoken about, and I won’t go into detail about it,’ White told talkSPORT. ‘Yes, it’s a decision that I don’t want to be talking about anyone negatively or throwing anyone under the bus.

Sheffield Wednesday need to rekindle transfer pursuit of Wrexham man in January following Dave Challinor arrival: Opinion

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Sheffield Wednesday were said to be keen on luring the player to Hillsborough in the last transfer window following their relegation to League One. However, nothing materialised in the end and the SWFC target stayed put at the Racecourse Ground beyond the last deadline in September.

The Owls should now rekindle their pursuit of the London-born man though in January following the appointment of Dave Challinor as their new head coach. They could do with some more quality in their squad and he would be ideal at Hillsborough.

Dobson, 28, has made nine appearances for Wrexham this season in all competitions and faces tough competition for game time from the likes of Lewis O’Brien, Ben Whiteman and Oli Rathbone, among others. Despite being under contract in North Wales until June 2028, he would benefit from a change of scene now to South Yorkshire.

Sheffield Wednesday are eyeing an immediate promotion back to the Championship under Challinor and are an exciting project to be a part of these days. They are sat in 9th in the table after 13 fixtures and they are two points off the play-offs behind Reading.

Dobson knows what it takes to get out of the third tier. He helped Wrexham go up in 2025 as runners-up under Phil Parkinson.

If he joined Sheffield Wednesday this winter, he would give them more competition and depth in the middle of the park. He could battle it out with Barry Bannan, Callum Slattery and Billy Mitchell for a place in Challinor’s starting XI.

Dobson is an experienced operator who has played over 400 games in his career to date. He would be a useful character for the Owls to have in and around the dressing room.

Prior to his switch to Wrexham, he had three years at Charlton Athletic. He has also had spells at West Ham, Sparta Rotterdam, Walsall, Sunderland and AFC Wimbledon in the past so is well travelled.

Challinor will want to put his own stamp on his newly inherited Sheffield Wednesday squad with some reinforcements. Dobson would fit the bill for SWFC and would get plenty of minutes there.

The Owls could provide him with a fresh start away from Wrexham. He could be a useful long-term replacement for Bannan, who at the age of 36 doesn’t have too many years left in the tank.

Challinor’s side lost 2-1 away at Oldham Athletic in the FA Cup last time out. Next up is an EFL Trophy tie against Accrington Stanley on Wednesday night at home.

10 Funniest 'Far Side' Comics About Dinosaurs That Prove Gary Larson Is a Mad Genius

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

On May 27, 1982, The Far Side cartoonist Gary Larson published what would become one of the most famous comic strips ever. The image features a classroom full of cavemen being taught by a caveman instructor, and the instructor points at an image of a Stegosaurus tail, specifically the spiky thing at the bottom of the tail. Larson's caption reads, “Now, this end is called the thagomizer … after the late Thag Simmons.”

On the surface, this might not seem like such a big deal. It's just one more of Larson’s many silly comic strips featuring cavemen. But this one ended up taking on a life of its own.

At the time this comic was published, that spiky apparatus at the end of a Stegosaurus’ tail actually had no name at all. So scientists began to jokingly use the word “thagomizer” to describe it. Eventually, the usage became so common that it came to be scientifically recognized and used by such distinguished institutions as the Smithsonian, confirming that one little Far Side joke made scientific dinosaur history.

But while that comic may be Larson’s most famous and historically significant dinosaur comic strip, it was far from his only one. Larson regularly drew dinosaurs in his cartoons, oftentimes doing normal human things or being completely unaware of their impending extinction. The Far Side ran from 1980 until 1995 and Larson created over 4,000 strips during that time. Dozens of them featured dinosaurs. And while only one made dinosaur history, there have been lots of unforgettable ones for Far Side fans. Here are 10 of the most unforgettable Far Side comics about dinosaurs (besides the thagomizer one).

Larson regularly depicted dinosaurs as doing normal human stuff, and the comedy came from these giant prehistoric monsters acting so calmly. Like this one, there’s nothing that funny about flossing, but when a T-rex talks about it, it’s hilarious.

This strip, about a juvenile T-rex looking for the prize in the bottom of a cereal box, is another one with dinosaurs acting like humans. The comedy is only enhanced here by the writing on the box, “Free kid inside!”

Here, Larson depicts a dinosaur press conference where one dinosaur declares that the climate is changing and mammals are taking over. It’s a really clear example of Larson’s other big dinosaur theme: extinction. A lot of Far Side comics are incredibly dark in their humor, and with dinosaurs, Larson seemed to love depicting them as doomed creatures, usually with no idea that their destruction is imminent.

In this woman’s defense, that does look a lot like a dryer.

This one, from August 9, 1985, is a bit of a play on the difficulty people have with dinosaur names. As a dinosaur nerd, though, I feel compelled to say that pterosaurs, like the one pictured here, are not dinosaurs. They are a completely separate group of reptiles which existed at the same time as dinosaurs, but are not dinosaurs themselves. Technically, it would be more accurate to put a comic strip featuring a chicken on this list, as birds are dinosaurs (and Larson did a ton of chicken comics).

Okay, I’ll add one chicken comic to this list, only because it’s also a dinosaur comic (and again, birds are dinosaurs).

Again, pterosaurs aren’t dinosaurs, but this joke, from January 18, 1986, is too funny to resist. It becomes all the funnier when you take a look at the limbs of the caveman in the back.

Some of Larson’s best dinosaur jokes are actually about the paleontologists, like this one. While people might expect paleontologists to be dull, sober scientists, Larson loved to depict them, and other scientists, as ridiculous children.

This is one of Larson’s best Far Side comics without any words. The visual of dinosaurs as schoolyard bullies, picking on a mammal for its fur, is funny enough, but the dinosaur in the back, noticing that it's snowing, gives the comic levels. Again, it's Larson poking fun at dinosaurs who have no idea their days are numbered.

Aside from the thagomizer comic, this has got to be Gary Larson’s most famous dinosaur image. From December 15, 1982, this absurd cartoon shows dinosaurs smoking cigarettes with the caption “The real reason dinosaurs became extinct.” The comic combines Larson’s two big dinosaur themes, in that it shows them doing normal human stuff and it’s about their extinction. It's also an image that is both smart and profoundly silly, like the very best Far Side comics.

BitGo revenue falls 51% in Q3 as trading slows; net income rises on treasury gains

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The revenue decline was driven mainly by lower digital asset sales amid a continued soft crypto trading environment and a higher mix of lower-margin stablecoin transactions, the company explained.

BitGo BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) saw a notable decline in revenue during the third quarter of this year, which it attributed to weaker digital asset trading activity.

According to its press release, BitGo reported Q3 revenue of $2.85 billion, down 50.9% from a year earlier and 34.1% from the prior quarter.

The revenue decline was driven mainly by lower digital asset sales, which fell 52% year-over-year and 35.2% sequentially amid a continued soft crypto trading environment and a higher mix of lower-margin stablecoin transactions. After direct costs, revenue was $37.3 million, down 32.1% from a year earlier.

Meanwhile, BitGo's net income rose to $44.1 million, up from $22.7 million last year. This growth was primarily driven by a $71.5 million unrealized gain on the company’s digital asset treasury. As of Sept. 30, the company held 2,399 BTC on its own balance sheet, valued at $200.4 million.

The company recorded an operating loss of $19.2 million and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $4.3 million.

"During the third quarter, we continued to strengthen BitGo's position as a leading institutional digital asset infrastructure platform," CEO Mike Belshe said in the statement.

Belshe cited the Go Network — the firm's institutional settlement and trading infrastructure — and its tokenized equity initiatives as some of the primary highlights in the quarter.

"We see significant opportunities to broaden access to U.S. equities through existing digital asset and stablecoin networks, while expanding the utility of traditional investment portfolios through on-chain settlement, collateral, and financing applications," Belshe added.

The company said normalized assets on the platform rose 32% year-over-year, reflecting continued institutional adoption when adjusted for price changes. It completed the acquisition of NYDIG's institutional trading business during the quarter and expanded off-exchange settlement connectivity, including with OKX. BitGo also repurchased $24.7 million of common stock under its $50 million authorization.

BitGo's NYSE-listed shares rose 4.94% to close at $5.10 on Monday and added another 2.9% in extended trading.

Findity and Suiteify partner to launch Suiteify Expense & Travel in Germany

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

STOCKHOLM, Nov. 10, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Findity, the leader in embedded expense management, and Suiteify (formerly Infoniqa), a provider of integrated business software for HR, payroll, finance and ERP for mid-sized companies in the DACH region, today announced a strategic partnership to launch Suiteify Expense & Travel in Germany.

Through this partnership, Suiteify embeds Findity's expense technology directly into its existing product suite, allowing customers to manage expenses seamlessly without leaving their everyday workflow. The native, AI-powered expense solution automates the full lifecycle, from real-time card transactions and receipt capture to mileage and per diems, eliminating manual reconciliation while ensuring local tax and GoBD compliance in Germany.

Patrick Olsson, CEO at Findity, said: "We are thrilled to partner with Suiteify to bring embedded expense management directly to German businesses. With Suiteify Expense & Travel, we are combining Suiteify's strong platform with our AI-driven expense technology to give their customers an automated, fully compliant experience that works seamlessly within their existing workflows."

"We chose Findity as our partner because the two companies complement each other; Findity brings a flexible, card-agnostic expense platform that we can build into our own product, and we bring the platform and the trust of more than 40,000 companies in the DACH region. Working together, we can offer German businesses an expense solution that feels like part of their everyday software from day one." Bjarne C. Wilhelm, SVP Product at Suiteify.

Suiteify Expense & Travel is now available to customers in Germany.

Suiteify offers an integrated business software suite for mid-sized companies in the DACH region, bringing HR, payroll, finance and ERP together in one system. More than 40,000 companies in Germany, Austria and Switzerland use Suiteify, drawing on more than 40 years of experience. For more information, visit suiteify.com.

Findity is the leader in embedded expense management. The company provides a card-agnostic, AI-powered expense platform, enabling partners to offer a localized and fully branded expense solution to their customers. Findity's headless architecture allows companies to build custom experiences using its Expense API or go to market instantly with a white-label solution, all powered by the same robust, end-to-end platform. Findity is the trusted technology behind the expense products of some of the world's most innovative companies. For more information, visit findity.com.

Lantronix: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Inventory was built to $31 million ahead of extended lead times, a move management tied to protecting supply for upcoming quarters.

On its first quarter of fiscal 2027 earnings call, held on Nov. 9, 2026, Lantronix (LTRX +5.52%) announced that first-quarter revenue grew 8% year over year to $32.4 million on strong demand for embedded edge computing. The compute hardware provider is expanding into high-margin software and specialized autonomous defense hardware, insulating its profitability from legacy network shifts. Three things management said stand out.

Lantronix is moving up the technology stack in unmanned aerial systems by consolidating discrete functions into single reference boards for autonomous drones and robotics. That hardware consolidation speeds design cycles for defense and industrial clients.

By acquiring Nero Global Tracking, a provider of fleet management tracking, Lantronix added high-margin recurring software revenue to its hardware base. Pro forma calculations, which show combined results as if the deal closed earlier, show services now exceed 10% of sales.

Demand from artificial intelligence data centers has constrained the availability of memory components across the embedded compute market. Management expanded net inventory to $31 million to secure system-on-module supplies, which are compact computing boards, and prevent delivery delays.

Demand across defense platforms and industrial vision is accelerating, and leadership is showcasing that momentum.

Federal trade compliance, hardware-software integration, and proprietary computer vision tuning give the business structural entry barriers. Leadership is watching component supply stability and pricing discipline while pushing for double-digit annual growth.

Top 25 EFL Championship goalscorers since the 2004 rebrand

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Goalscorers are often the difference between a Championship side merely competing and one capable of going up, which makes them among the most valuable commodities in the game. Football League World have looked at the top 25 since the inception of the division in 2004.

Of course, a reliable striker can turn draws into wins, settle tight games, and provide the consistency required over a gruelling 46-game season. However, the problem for Championship clubs is that when one of those forwards hits the ground running, they do not always get to enjoy the benefits for very long.

A prolific season in the second tier can put a player firmly on the radar of top flight clubs looking for a proven goalscorer, while the alternative is often just as painful for the Championship club when the striker scores enough goals to help fire them to promotion and then faces a new challenge in the top flight.

It is little surprise, then, that centre-forwards regularly command some of the biggest transfer fees and salaries in football. There have been plenty of prolific names in the Championship since the division was rebranded ahead of the 2004/05 season, with some strikers establishing themselves as second-tier specialists and others using it as a springboard towards the Premier League.

Some of the division's most exciting modern goalscorers are not yet eligible for this particular list because they simply have not spent long enough in the Championship. Middlesbrough's Will Lankshear, Wolverhampton Wanderers' Raul Jimenez, West Ham's Taty Castellanos, and Southampton's Cyle Larin are among the forwards currently capable of making their mark, but their Championship stories are only just beginning.

But who has actually scored the most goals since the Championship era began? Including play-off goals as well as goals scored during the regular league season? We have taken a look and ranked the top 25, here.

His Championship career took Martyn Waghorn to a number of clubs, including Leicester City, Ipswich Town, Derby County, and Coventry City. He was particularly effective during his time at Ipswich, where his goals made him one of the division's leading forwards. His 78 goals put him just ahead of Britt Assombalonga and David McGoldrick below him in the standings, but there are the likes of Kieffer Moore and Ben Brereton-Diaz closing in on his tally from the crop of currently active players.

Joel Piroe has established himself as one of the Championship's most reliable modern goalscorers, reaching 78 goals during spells with Swansea City, Leeds United, and now West Ham United. The Dutchman has been particularly prolific in the second tier and remains an active player capable of climbing this list further. Piroe will hope more goals can help the Hammers achieve their ambitions this season, with his 78 goals just ahead of Waghorn on a goal-per-game basis.

Across England's top two divisions, Chris Wood has enjoyed an outstanding career, with his Championship goals coming for the likes of Leicester, Leeds, Millwall, Ipswich, and Birmingham City. His 78 goals match Waghorn's tally as well as Piroe's, but his superior goals-per-minute ratio means he sits higher than both. Wood has not played a minute of Championship football since leaving Leeds for Burnley and has gone on to score 92 goals in the Premier League as well.

One of the Championship's most experienced forwards during the 2000s and early 2010s, Marlon King scored regularly for clubs including Watford, Coventry, and Birmingham. He eventually reached 80 goals in the division, underlining his consistency across a lengthy second-tier career which began prior to the Championship's rebranding. King's physical presence and eye for goal made him a valuable Championship striker for many a year.

Premier League pedigree did not prevent Kevin Phillips from continuing to score at an impressive rate in the Championship. After his famous goalscoring exploits with Sunderland, Phillips found the net regularly for clubs including West Bromwich Albion, Birmingham, Blackpool, Crystal Palace, and Leicester. His 82 Championship goals highlight that his finishing ability remained intact well beyond his top-flight peak and well into his 30s.

During spells with the likes of Watford, Middlesbrough, and Blackburn Rovers, Danny Graham became one of the Championship's most dependable goalscorers. His movement and finishing made him a consistent threat for years, while his goals helped earn him opportunities in the Premier League. Graham's 83 goals leave him inside the division's top 20 scorers since the 2004/05 rebrand.

Peter Whittingham was one of the defining Championship players of his generation, spending much of his career with Cardiff City after an earlier spell at Aston Villa. His left foot, set-piece ability, and eye for goal made him an influential attacking midfielder or winger rather than a conventional striker. His 84 goals are an impressive return for a player operating primarily from midfield. Although he was an attacking player, he is one of only two midfielders on this list.

Dexter Blackstock never scored more than 13 in a season and only hit double figures three times as well, but consistency rather than one spectacular scoring season was the hallmark of his Championship career. He represented clubs including QPR and Nottingham Forest, becoming a familiar figure across the division. Blackstock's 84 goals put him level with Whittingham, although his goals-per-minute record places him slightly better on this particular ranking.

After scoring a few for Newcastle United and then joining Fulham, Aleksandar Mitrovic wasted little time establishing himself as one of the Championship's most fearsome strikers. He scored for Newcastle before his remarkable Fulham spell produced a record-breaking individual season and helped secure promotion. His 85 goals are a remarkable total considering how much of his career has been spent outside the Championship. He has the best goal-per-minute ratio of any player on the list.

FLW have ranked the 10 best EFL Championship stadiums for atmosphere so far in the 2026/27 season.

A remarkably long Championship career saw Cameron Jerome represent clubs including Cardiff, Birmingham, Norwich City, and Derby County. His pace and power made him difficult to contain, while his longevity saw him accumulate 86 goals. Jerome's ability to contribute across multiple clubs, as well as many generations of Championship football, makes his tally particularly impressive.

FIGC, Galliani e Malagò: «un’elezione nuovamente annullabile»

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Il legale Lattanzi chiede di sospendere il provvedimento: le verifiche tornerebbero al Segretario Generale.

Adriano Galliani ha presentato ricorso al Tribunale Federale Nazionale della FIGC contro l’istituzione della commissione incaricata di esaminare le candidature alla presidenza federale. Secondo quanto riportato da Onefootball, La commissione è stata voluta dal commissario Giovanni Malagò, a sua volta in corsa. La contestazione riguarda anche il sostegno del Genoa: se la firma del club a favore di Malagò fosse annullata, il commissario non raggiungerebbe la quota minima richiesta tra le società di Serie A.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il ricorso mette in discussione l’affidamento delle verifiche alla commissione istituita su iniziativa di Malagò. I dettagli, riferiti da Gianluca Di Marzio e ripresi dall’ANSA, sono riportati da Onefootball.

Secondo il legale Lattanzi, che in mattinata ha rassegnato le dimissioni dalla commissione agenti FIGC, il ricorso richiede un intervento prima che il procedimento elettorale prosegua. Lattanzi sostiene che il pregiudizio per Galliani, legato alla possibilità di competere ad armi pari e di ottenere una valutazione imparziale delle candidature, non sarebbe sanabile successivamente.

Al centro del reclamo c’è la duplice sottoscrizione del Genoa. Il club aveva inizialmente sostenuto Galliani, che aveva inserito quell’adesione negli atti formalizzati. In seguito ha cambiato posizione, schierandosi a favore di Malagò attraverso una PEC di revoca e diffida.

L’eventuale annullamento del sostegno del Genoa a Malagò avrebbe un effetto sulla sua candidatura: senza quella firma, il commissario non arriverebbe alle undici sottoscrizioni minime dei club di Serie A. Il termine per presentare le candidature è fissato al 14 novembre.

After Maatsen Agreement, Aston Villa Must Agree New Deal with 'Fantastic' Onana

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Aston Villa are said to be closing in on a new contract agreement after striking a 'verbal agreement' with Ian Maatsen, but they must now turn their attention to Amadou Onana.

Unai Emery's men endured a difficult weekend having led 4-2 against Manchester United only to concede twice late on and draw 4-4.It was a sloppy finale from Villa, and Emery was left raging at the full-time whistle. That result followed an away defeat to Barcelona, so it's fair to say Villa go into the international break with a bitter taste in their mouths.

But there is some good news emerging on Monday, with Football Insider reporting that the club have verbally agreed a new contract with Maatsen, who has been a regular this season. The 24-year-old was already tied down until 2030, but Villa are now set to reward him with fresh terms and an extended deal, which is a wise move given his age.

Maatsen has stepped up following the exit of Lucas Digne, and most Villa fans are likely to be in agreement with the new deal when it is finalised.

Villa are taking care of business with Maatsen, and it's believed they are close to announcing a new deal for Emiliano Buendia, whose current deal expires in the summer. Ross Barkley could be in line for a new deal ahead of his deal expiring in the summer, while there will be decisions to be made over John McGinn and Pau Torres, whose deals expire in the summer of 2028, when both players will be North of 30.

Though Pau Torres has already confirmed that he expects to rejoin hometown club Villarreal. He said earlier this year:

"If nothing strange happens, I will complete the two years I have left on my contract and that will be the end of my time at Aston Villa."

"What I would like is for Villarreal not to have to pay anything for me, to arrive as a free agent. It is the club of my life and I do not want them spending money to bring me back. If, when that moment comes, the coach at the club thinks I can provide what is needed, I would be delighted to return. It is something I have in mind and I have spoken about it with the president."

That likely means a new deal for Torres is not on the table, and so Villa should turn their attention to Onana, whose current deal is up in 2029. The midfielder has been vital to Emery's midfield, and his absence this season through a long-term injury has been felt. Onana said of the midfielder back in February: "His potential is still there, and when he is playing, being ready and 100 per cent, his performances are fantastic. He has progressed a lot since being here for a year and a half."

Onana only recently turned 25 years of age, so there is no real concern over the long-term impacts of his injury, and given his importance, Villa don't want to find themselves in a situation where the Belgian has reduced time remaining on his contract and a top club comes and picks him off in Youri Tielemans style, albeit he had a release clause in his deal.

Villa won't be in a rush, given Onana is dealing with a long term issue, but when they look at their list of to-dos on the contract front, the midfielder is likely to be close to the top of it. And while it's a business way of thinking, Onana is likely to appreciate a longer term commitment without any significant wage boost at a time when he is coming back from a serious injury. That length commitment may be sufficient to strike a deal.

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Know what you're getting each month with my comparison

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I don't know about you, but I am constantly hitting my token usage limits at the worst times. And it seems like whichever model I use, I get stuck much sooner than anticipated. Although AI coding assistants promise to take the hard work out of programming, it seems like the catch is that paying $20 a month doesn't mean you can keep coding for as long as you want. Unlike ChatGPT's unlimited free chatting, coding limitations are actually pretty strict.

Because I was so tired of hitting the wall, I decided to compare which subscription offers better value, particularly for someone who codes regularly but doesn't want to pay $200 a month.

Rather than burn through two subscriptions with unexpected coding challenges, I compared the companies' published usage policies, pricing and available independent research.

What I found is that neither company guarantees a fixed number of coding tasks for $20, and a major change coming to ChatGPT could make that decision even more important. Here's a closer look.

On paper, the subscriptions look very similar. ChatGPT Plus costs $20 per month and includes Codex alongside the regular ChatGPT experience. Claude Pro also costs $20 per month and provides access to Claude Code alongside Claude's chatbot.

Both subscriptions impose 5-hour usage limits and weekly allowances. The key difference is how those allowances work and how clearly users can estimate their remaining usage.

OpenAI publishes estimated message ranges for Codex models, while Anthropic explains that Claude's limits depend on factors including conversation length, model selection and task complexity. In other words, Anthropic remains completely vague about how and when you'll hit your limit.

Sign up to the Tom's AI Guide weekly newsletter summing up all the biggest AI news you need to know. Plus, analysis from our AI editors and tips on how to use the latest AI tools!

Neither 5-hour limit means that much time of uninterrupted coding. You can use up your allowance before the window ends, which I have done nearly every day since subscribing.

One advantage Codex has is that OpenAI publishes estimated usage ranges for different models. I have found the company's current usage documentation helpful. Knowing what to expect is at least a starting point. From this, I have surmised that ChatGPT Plus users can expect approximately 5 to 45 local messages using GPT-6 Astra within a 5-hour window. Knowing this has helped me manage my token budget much better. For example, switching to GPT-6.1 Sol increases that estimated range to 15 to 160 messages, while GPT-6 Luna has an estimated range of 350 to 3,000 messages. Clearly, this is an enormous difference depending on which model you choose.

There are caveats, of course. These aren't guaranteed message counts, and a complex task involving multiple steps can consume considerably more usage than a simple request. Cloud tasks may also use more allowance than local messages.

Still, publishing these ranges gives users a better starting point when deciding which model to use.

Claude Code operates under a similar system, but Anthropic doesn't provide an equivalent universal table of guaranteed coding tasks for its $20 Pro subscription.

Instead, it explains that usage depends on the model, conversation length, features and complexity of the work. That makes it difficult — nearly impossible — to predict exactly how far $20 will take you with either platform.

There's another factor worth considering: how many tokens the assistants consume while completing tasks. A Zapier comparison cites a February 2026 benchmark involving a Figma-to-code task.

What matters is how much useful work gets completed before the subscription limit is reached. I find myself hitting a limit much sooner with Claude Code.

According to the comparison, Claude Code consumed approximately 6.2 million tokens while Codex used 1.5 million, a difference of more than four times.

However, Claude Code reportedly produced a more thorough result. That matters because lower token consumption doesn't automatically mean better value if the output requires more work afterward.

More recent data also reinforces how differently developers use these tools. An analysis from whoburnedmore published recently examined reported usage across an opt-in group of 229 developers over 30 days.

Claude Code accounted for 57% of observed tokens, compared with 35% for Codex CLI.

But it's good to note that this wasn't a controlled test of identical coding tasks, and the sample wasn't representative of all developers. It shows how much the participating developers used each tool, not which assistant is more efficient.

The takeaway is that token counts alone aren't enough to declare a winner. What matters is how much useful work gets completed before the subscription limit is reached. In my experience, I find myself hitting a limit much sooner with Claude Code.

Here's something anyone considering either subscription should understand. With Claude Pro, usage is shared across Claude's chatbot and Claude Code. This means, if you've been spending the morning researching, analyzing documents or working through a long conversation in Claude, that activity can reduce the allowance available for coding.

Anthropic confirms that usage across its supported Claude interfaces contributes to the same limits.

Codex has a similar consideration, although the structure is changing. Currently, Codex and ChatGPT Work share an allowance, with five-hour and weekly limits applying to Plus users.

But OpenAI says that starting in December 2026, Plus, Pro and Business subscriptions will move toward a shared ChatGPT and Codex usage allowance.

The company says usage will be measured in tokens, with consumption depending on the model and task. Instant chat won't draw from that allowance.

Neither subscription necessarily leaves you without options when you run out of included usage. It's all too tempting to pay for additional usage credits, which switch eligible requests to pay-as-you-go pricing after the subscription allowance is exhausted. But hopefully by understanding what you get, you won't have to now.

The important point is that paying $20 doesn't guarantee unlimited coding with either tool.

If you routinely work on complex applications, large repositories or lengthy agent-driven tasks, you should expect to encounter limits sooner than someone who only occasionally asks for a bug fix.

After comparing the published policies and my own usage, I'd give Codex the edge for users who want more visibility into their usage, particularly because OpenAI provides estimated message ranges for its different models.

Claude Code remains a compelling choice, especially for users who prefer its workflow or consistently get better results from Claude's models. Personally, I find it the more effective option the majority of the time for my projects. But if your biggest concern is paying for an assistant that suddenly tells you to come back later, both products require some planning.

For occasional coding, either $20 subscription could be enough. For more intensive work, the models you select, the complexity of your projects and your other AI activity can have a major impact on how quickly those limits disappear.

The Nintendo Switch 2 Can No Longer Be Jailbroken

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sam Woods is TheGamer’s Senior Reporter. He’s been in games journalism since 2020 and has undertaken a variety of roles in that time. These have included roles such as SEO Editor, Managing Editor at DualShockers, and Global Head of Gaming, and he's also been seen on the BBC. He was promoted to the Senior Reporter position in 2025 following a series of high-quality reports and exclusives for TheGamer.

Since 2020, Sam has written over 3,000 articles and specializes in telling the stories of the people behind the games. He’s interviewed the biggest game directors, some of the industry’s top talents, told the stories of how indie games came to be, and spoken to players about what really makes them tick. He’s also interviewed some of the world’s biggest musical artists to find out how the medium has inspired their work, and TV and Film actors about their roles in video game adaptations. He even hosted a PAX panel in 2021 and made a documentary in 2024 surrounding the creation of a Disney game.

Alongside this, Sam focuses on breaking news. He got the scoop on Perfect Dark’s cancellation, provided context on some AI drama, and revealed that Lady Dimitrescu’s actress would love to step back into the character’s giant shoes. These articles have been picked up by the biggest gaming sites, including IGN, GameSpot, and PC Gamer. He also loves to dive into the numbers behind the games.

Sam’s journey in games journalism has taken him all over the world. He’s covered Gamescom in Germany, Summer Game Fest in Los Angeles, and Tokyo Game Show in Japan, each on three occasions.

He’s been learning Japanese since 2024 with the intention of identifying and translating Japanese-language news and bringing it to English-language audiences first, something he’s done countless times.

Sam’s first review was 2020’s Battletoads reboot, and he’s since reviewed games in The Legend of Zelda, Super Mario, and Pokemon franchises.

Late last month, the Nintendo Switch 2 was jailbroken for the first time. Modders, with the help of AI, found an exploit in a game called SmileBASIC that let them bypass many of the restrictions on Nintendo's latest hardware. It ultimately led to a heavily modded version of Mario Kart World, with characters like Cinnamoroll, Peter Griffin, and Hatsune Miku racing against the likes of Mario, Donkey Kong, and Dry Bones.

It was an early warning sign for Nintendo that its hardware wasn't as safe as the company must have thought, so naturally, they've moved to fix it, with today's Nintendo Switch 2 software update patching out the exploit.

Today, Nintendo released the Switch 2's Ver. 23.5.0 update. This update came with a range of changes seen below:

While gifting games is a fantastic addition, the patch notes don't mention that the jailbreak exploit has been removed. That was, instead, first spotted by OatmealDome on Twitter.

They claim that, at least for the time being, "Dumping and modding games will be impossible". They say there will be exceptions from some games releasing in the future, but for now, the loop is closed.

They add, "While game exploits like SmileBASIC and Civilization VII still exist, it is no longer possible to 'escape' the game starting with 23.5.0. This effectively means that the capabilities of homebrew are locked to whatever the game can do already."

It's no surprise that Nintendo got on top of this one, especially given the situation it's facing with The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time on PC.

Simple fungi can recognize shapes, like circles and crosses, when looking for wood to eat

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Food  |  Read Source →

Daily Wonder, Pink Oyster Mushroom A fungus has no brain, no eyes, and no nerves. Yet a wood-rotting fungus, given a cross of wood blocks to grow on,…

A fungus has no brain, no eyes, and no nerves. Yet a wood-rotting fungus, given a cross of wood blocks to grow on, attached more of its threads to the outer blocks than to the inner ones.

Given a circle to grow on, it showed no such pattern and left the center bare. The researchers suggested the difference may be a form of pattern recognition.

Yu Fukasawa of Tohoku University in Japan led the 2024 study with colleagues at Tohoku and at Nagaoka College.

The fungus they used lives on forest floors and builds its networks by sending thick strands of thread, called cords, from one piece of dead wood to the next.

Its name is Phanerochaete velutina, and Fukasawa’s team had earlier found that it remembers the direction to a piece of wood.

“You’d be surprised at just how much fungi are capable of,” Fukasawa said. “They have memories, they learn, and they can make decisions. Quite frankly, the differences in how they solve problems compared to humans is mind-blowing.”

Even so, the team phrased its own conclusion as a question, not a verdict.

The mushroom in the video above is a pink oyster, not the fungus in this study. Pink oysters, named for their salmon-pink caps, grow on dead hardwood and are farmed on straw as food.

The study’s fungus grows as a thin crust on wood, with no cap or stem. Both break down dead wood with a network of threads.

Fukasawa’s team cut beech wood into cubes less than half an inch (1 cm) on a side, weighed them, and let the fungus grow into them for six weeks.

The team then set nine cubes on each tray of damp soil, in a circle or in a cross, with 2 inches (5 cm) between neighboring blocks. Each tray was about 9.5 inches (24 cm) square, and there were ten of each shape.

Everything but the shape was the same from tray to tray. The trays stayed in the dark at 68°F (20°C) for 116 days, nearly four months.

Once a week the team misted them with sterile water and moved them around the incubator. A camera 18 inches (46 cm) above the trays photographed each one monthly.

At the end, the researchers counted the cords attached to each block, then dried and weighed the blocks again to see how much wood the fungus had broken down.

After 116 days, cords ran from block to block across every tray, though a few blocks ended up with no cord attached at all.

In the cross, the four blocks at the ends of the arms had more cords than the four middle blocks and the one at the center. The count ran from zero cords on some blocks to nine on others.

In the circle, no position in the ring had reliably more cords than another. The cross result was the opposite of what the team had predicted.

They had expected the center block, the one with four neighbors, to be the most connected, and instead the blocks at the ends of the arms were.

The team suggested the outer blocks may have worked as outposts, where the fungus absorbed water and nutrients from the soil. The inner blocks, with fewer cords, may have become little more than a path for cords passing through.

The more cords a block had, the more of its wood the fungus had broken down. Blocks in the circle lost between 18% and 65.5% of their dry weight, and blocks in the cross lost between 16.7% and 62.7%. Overall, the cross blocks lost a little less than the circle blocks did.

The researchers also reran the analysis without the extreme blocks, the ones with no cords or with nine. Without them, the difference between the two shapes faded, while the link between cords and decay remained.

The fungus also spread roughly the same amount of thread over the soil in both shapes. So the cross blocks didn’t decay less because the fungus grew less. The network was built differently, and the wood decayed differently.

The recognition argument rests on that difference. In a human brain, different images activate different groups of neurons, and that contrast in activity is what lets the brain recognize an image.

A network of fungal threads that builds itself one way for a cross and another for a circle may be doing something similar, Fukasawa’s team wrote.

Every block started the same way, with threads growing outward in all directions. Then the fronts from neighboring blocks met and fused into one body.

In the circle trays, the merged fungus kept growing outward, and by day 34 a large bare patch had formed at the center.

Fukasawa’s team compared this to fairy rings, the circles of mushrooms that widen year after year because the fungus beneath them grows outward from its edge. Why the fused network left the center empty is something the team can’t yet explain.

One possibility they raised is crowding: fungal growth is known to stop once the threads get dense enough, and the threads inside the ring may have reached that point.

Whether the fungus is conscious is irrelevant, the team wrote, because cognition can run without consciousness even in a brain.

Slime molds, which also have no brain, have already solved a maze and linked food sources with networks about as efficient as a real railway.

The team raised one possible explanation, and it involves rhythm. The amount of calcium at each growing tip rises and falls in a cycle, and so do the flow of material and the electrical activity through the fungus.

When separate colonies fuse, those rhythms tend to synchronize, and synchronized rhythms may be how the network carries information about where things are.

The rhythm idea is a hypothesis, and this experiment didn’t measure it. The team also can’t yet say whether the tray lids affected the empty center of the ring.

The lids were loose and came off every week, so the researchers doubt the middle ran short of oxygen. The way to settle it is a tray with no lid, and that’s the experiment they said would be worth running.

The full study was published in the journal Fungal Ecology.

The Grand Canyon’s drinking water comes from a single spring connected to over six miles of hidden caves

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Researchers mapped over six miles of caves beneath the Grand Canyon, revealing how groundwater flows and why its drinking water faces pollution risks.

For decades, the twelve different water spigots used by visitors in Grand Canyon National Park have drawn their water from Roaring Springs, a cave-fed spring on the North Rim.

In an August 2025 study, researchers at Northern Arizona University (NAU) described over 6 miles (10 kilometers) of cave passages behind the spring. They mapped them with a handheld lidar scanner.

The 3D maps reveal the passages that carry water through the limestone below the North Rim. That system is fast and vulnerable to contamination from the surface.

The park’s water supply has served more than 6 million visitors a year from that single cave spring. Its water is almost entirely snowmelt from the Kaibab Plateau.

Built starting in 1965, the 12½-mile (20-kilometer) Transcanyon Waterline carried that water toward the South Rim and its 800-plus historic buildings.

The aging pipe had at least 85 major breaks between 2010 and mid-2026, according to the National Park Service. A single break often tops $25,000 to repair.

A replacement project shifts the South Rim intake to Bright Angel Creek near Phantom Ranch. On August 29, 2026, a flash flood destroyed a water intake pump and other infrastructure.

The Bright Angel Creek and Roaring Springs intake areas “sustained significant damage,” the park reported in a September 2026 update. Roughly 40 percent of the old pipeline between Phantom Ranch and the North Rim was also damaged.

The South Rim has been under Stage 4 water restrictions while the park arranged to truck in about 200,000 gallons (757,000 liters) a day. Pumping from Bright Angel Creek to a new treatment plant could begin by November 16.

Blase LaSala, a Ph.D. student in ecoinformatics at NAU’s School of Informatics, Computing, and Cyber Systems, led the mapping. He worked with NAU professor Temuulen “Teki” Sankey, a remote sensing expert.

With park staff and volunteers, the team scanned Roaring Springs Cave, Muav West Cave and Redwall East Cave. The handheld scanner uses near-infrared laser light and records up to 300,000 points per second.

The crews documented more than 6.3 miles (10.2 kilometers) of passages in 25 days. The caves extend beyond 0.6 miles (1 kilometer) from their entrances and 1,300 feet (400 meters) below them.

In Muav West Cave, the lidar maps agreed 98 percent with a traditional survey, which covered about half the distance in the same time.

“I had no idea how large and long these caves are,” Sankey said. “Grand Canyon’s caves have never been mapped in 3D like this.”

Karst forms where water dissolves soluble rock such as limestone, opening caves, conduits and sinkholes.

The caves lie in the Redwall and Muav limestones, karst layers some 2,300 feet (700 meters) beneath the plateau surface. Water travels through these limestone layers via steep fractures and underground conduits.

Abe Springer, an NAU professor and co-author, also worked with the park on dye tracing tests. Dye dropped into surface sinkholes reached springs about 12 miles (20 kilometers) away in as little as a week.

From the 3D models, the authors conclude that today’s groundwater follows near-vertical cracks and the tilt of the rock layers. They link those patterns to regional faulting and uplift.

In Redwall East Cave, bubble trails and other features sit above the stream passage. They suggest that today’s flow may reuse pathways carved by an older aquifer.

“It’s like looking at a black box,” LaSala explained. “You see what comes in and what comes out, but it’s very hard to quantify what’s going on in there.”

The researchers describe karst aquifers like this one as both vulnerable to contamination and challenging to model.

Runoff from burned areas or E. coli bacteria entering sinkholes linked to Roaring Springs Cave could force the park to halt its pumps, NAU warns.

The Dragon Bravo Fire, which started on July 4, 2025, burned 149,399 acres (60,460 hectares), including 71,129 acres (28,785 hectares) of the park.

The park’s burned area emergency response listed protection of the drinking water system among its treatments.

The fire also changed conditions on the Kaibab Plateau, where the snowmelt that feeds the spring begins. The NAU team now has to account for those effects.

“It’s a new twist to our study,” Sankey noted.

A new grant from Grand Canyon National Park will expand the mapping across the park. The university scheduled that work to start in early 2026.

LaSala and Sankey plan to use airborne lidar and decades of satellite data to map sinkholes on both sides of the canyon. They will also track how snowmelt has accumulated over the last 40 years.

“Understanding where the water sinks is critical for the infrastructure, the animals, the plants and the rest of the ecosystems that rely on these springs,” LaSala said. “They’re like oases.”

The study was published in the journal Scientific Reports.
