Next Manchester United manager odds as club make decision on Michael Carrick

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As reports emerge that Michael Carrick is set to be sacked as Manchester United manager, a new favourite to replace him has emerged.

The Red Devils have had an abysmal start to the season under Carrick, winning just one of their 10 Premier League games to date.

That solitary victory came over Ipswich Town, with every other team in the division winning more matches than the Old Trafford giants at this stage.

There has not been much respite in cup competitions either, with an early exit from the Carabao Cup at the hands of Brighton and six points from four games in the Champions League.

Last week’s victory over Roma was the highlight of the season so far, but that 3-2 win only came after falling 2-0 down early in the piece.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

There is a general consensus that Carrick cannot be blamed entirely for the woeful results, but The Athletic report that he is still going to lose his job as a result and is expected to be dismissed during the current international break.

A number of possible candidates to replace him have been mooted in recent weeks, but a different name is now the shortest price with the bookmakers to be next Manchester United manager.

It was believed that the leading candidates were Eddie Howe, Julian Naglesmann and Antonio Conte, but Mauricio Pochettino is now odds-on favourite to be Manchester United’s next manager.

The 54-year-old is in charge of USA, having moved into international management in 2024.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

After the Americans lost in the last 16 of the World Cup to Belgium over the summer Pochettino was given a new contract, which runs until 2030.

Despite this, the bookmakers reckon he would swap The Land of the Free for Old Trafford and replace Carrick if he is given the chance.

The Argentine has plenty of Premier League experience having managed Southampton, Tottenham and Chelsea, although did not lift any silverware during any of those stints.

Pochettino spent a season-and-a-half at Paris Saint-Germain in 2021-22, winning Ligue 1 and the Cope de France.

The familiar trio of Howe, Naglesmann and Conte are behind Pochettino in the odds, with former Newcastle boss Howe considered the most likely of the three to get the Red Devils gig.

Other contenders are thought to be long shots, with names like Gareth Southgate, Didier Deschamps, Kieran McKenna and Unai Emery considered possibilities but unlikely.

Manchester United’s next outing is against Liverpool at Anfield on November 22 when a new manager is expected to have a feisty start to his new role.

‘Huge asset’: Birmingham City summer recruit lauded by ex-EFL player after eye-catching season so far

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Birmingham City signed the attacker on loan from Premier League side Hull City in the last transfer window. He has since made a positive impression with BCFC in the Championship and is due to spend the whole season with them.

Millar, 27, has made 11 appearances for the Blues in all competitions since his switch to the Midlands and he has chipped in with four goals and three assists. He is under contract at the MKM Stadium until June 2027, although the Tigers hold an option to extend his stay by 12 months until 2028.

Former Luton Town, Ipswich Town and Swindon Town man Parkin has delivered his verdict on the forward on Sky Sports’ The EFL Show: “That’s one hell of a signing considering he’s already scored a Premier League goal for his previous club Hull at the start of the season so to get him in and to start in the way he has with the numbers, goals and assists, he’s going to be a huge asset for them this year in their pursuit of promotion.”

Birmingham City’s decision to bring in Millar has turned out to be a masterstroke. He has settled in to life at BCFC with ease.

The Canada international, who has 47 caps to his name, gives the Blues more competition and depth in their attacking department. He is quick, tricky and can cause real problems to opposition defenders.

Millar was given the green light to leave Hull City on a temporary basis following their promotion to the top flight. He was pushed down the pecking order after they signed Sorba Thomas from Stoke City.

The Toronto-born man joined the Tigers back in 2024 after spells at Liverpool, Charlton Athletic, FC Basel and Preston North End. He went on to play 53 matches for the East Yorkshire club but only found the net on five occasions.

Birmingham City are likely to pursue a permanent move for Millar with the way things are going. He has become one of BCFC’s main players, as Parkin has alluded to.

The Blues’ chances of securing a full-time swoop will depend on what league they are in. If Chris Davies’ side go up, that would significantly boost their hopes.

Millar’s future will also be affected by where Hull City are. They are 13th in the Premier League after their first 10 outings at the top level.

Birmingham City won 2-1 at home against Swansea City last time out with Jay Stansfield scoring twice. They are back in action after the international break with an away trip to Stoke City as they aim to keep their momentum going.

The Blues find themselves at the summit after 15 fixtures. They are above Middlesbrough, Stoke City and Wolves.

FDA warns of vision loss risk linked to popular GLP-1 drugs like Wegovy, Zepbound

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The U.S. Food and Drug Administration is requiring new safety warnings on all GLP-1 drugs after finding evidence of a potential increased risk of a rare eye condition, the agency said on Monday.

The condition, which can cause sudden vision loss, known as nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION), is rare, and a causal relationship has not been established.

The FDA is requiring the makers of all GLP-1 medications to add a warning about the potential increased risk of NAION associated with use of these medications, it said.

NAION is a condition that can result in sudden decreased vision, including permanent vision loss. It is caused by decreased blood flow to the front part of the ​optic nerve — the underlying cause is not completely understood, however.

NAION cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 products, the FDA said.

CNBC has reached out to Ozempic and Wegovy maker Novo Nordisk and Eli Lilly, the company behind Mounjaro, Zepbound and Foundayo, for comment.

The FDA decision follows a review of clinical trials, real-world patient data, and published studies.

An analysis of 126 trials involving more than 134,000 patients found 6.4 cases of NAION per 100,000 patient-years among GLP-1 users, compared with 3.8 among patients receiving other treatments or placebos.

Several observational studies also identified an increased risk, supporting the FDA's concerns.

The FDA's class-wide labeling action is "likely to attract attention but should have limited impact on GLP-1 uptake," according to Citi analysts.

It may temporarily increase patient and investor concern, but the rarity of NAION and substantial weight, glycemic, cardiovascular, renal, and sleep apnea benefits should preserve a favorable benefit-risk profile and limit changes to prescribing the medicines, they added.

While GLP-1 drugs have been shown to lead to benefits beyond weight loss and controlling blood sugar levels, many also carry the FDA's strongest safety warning, known as a boxed warning, over a potential risk of thyroid tumors.

Studies have shown that semaglutide, the active ingredient in Wegovy and Ozempic, and tirzepatide, the active ingredient in Mounjaro, cause thyroid C-cell tumors in rodents. It remains unknown whether the drugs cause these tumors, including a rare form of thyroid cancer, in humans.

Michael Carrick arrives early to face his fate: Man United manager could be sacked TODAY - as he drives into training at 7.15am... with Mauricio Pochettino frontrunner to take over

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

By CHRIS WHEELER, NORTHERN SPORTS WRITER and NATHAN SALT - FOOTBALL REPORTER

Published: 02:40 EST, 10 November 2026 | Updated: 03:34 EST, 10 November 2026

Michael Carrick arrived at Manchester United's training ground early on Tuesday, checking in at 7.15am on the day he could face the sack.

Manchester United bosses are leaning towards installing a new head coach during the international break with the manager's position increasingly precarious after they dropped into the relegation zone despite salvaging a 4-4 draw against Aston Villa at Old Trafford on Sunday.

It left the 45-year-old with just one wins from ten Premier League games this season – United’s worst start since Sir Alex Ferguson replaced Ron Atkinson 40 years ago.

Carrick is expected to take training as usual this morning - with a cut-down group of players as a number of United's stars have departed on international duty.

Club chiefs including CEO Omar Berrada and director of football Jason Wilcox are expected to have a video call with co-owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe today.

Mauricio Pochettino has emerged as a new frontrunner to replace Carrick. The Argentine is currently in charge of the US national team, but his contract includes a £7million release clause.

Eddie Howe and Julian Nagelsmann, who were both under consideration when United gave Carrick more time to turn the season around following a 3-0 home defeat by Bournemouth last month, could also be in contention.

Michael Carrick is pictured arriving at Manchester United's training ground at 7.15am

Mauricio Pochettino is the frontrunner to replace Carrick - leaving his role as US head coach

In the three games since then, Carrick’s side lost at Chelsea, staged a remarkable comeback to beat Roma in the Champions League in midweek and then fought back again to draw with Villa.

Although it’s understood that no final decision has been reached over Carrick’s future and insiders say that speculation over his sacking is premature, the expectation within Old Trafford is that a new man will be in charge for the visit to Anfield a week on Sunday.

Carrick replaced Ruben Amorim in mid-January and the former United midfielder was handed a two-year contract in May after guiding United to a third-place finish and qualification for the Champions League, losing just two of his 17 games as head coach.

But this season has unravelled alarmingly for United with his only two wins in 15 games in all competitions coming at home to newly promoted Ipswich and Sabah of Azerbaijan.

Asked after Sunday’s chaotic draw with Villa if he would consider quitting, a defiant Carrick said: ‘That's not going to happen.

‘We’re in November. I don’t like this situation and where we are. But we can do something about it. I'm quite clear with what direction the club needs to go in, and it's got to win games.’

Avram Glazer and Sir Jim Ratcliffe, co-owners, are pictured at Old Trafford in September

Sacking Carrick would leave United searching for their eighth permanent manager or head coach since Ferguson retired 13-and-a-half years ago.

Pochettino was strongly linked with the job when Erik ten Hag was appointed in 2022 and again when the Dutchman was sacked two years later.

The 54-year-old has extensive Premier League experience at Tottenham, Chelsea and Southampton and was also in charge at Paris Saint-Germain.

Howe was previously seen as the favourite but it’s understood there has been no contact between United and the former Newcastle boss.

It would cost United around £3million in compensation to appoint the 48-year-old under the terms of his departure from St James’s Park in July.

Nagelsmann is available after leaving his role as head coach of the German national team in the summer when they were eliminated from the World Cup following a penalty shootout defeat by Paraguay in the round of 32.

Balmain Resort 2027 Menswear

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

“For me, the men’s and the women’s, they are kind of like one entity. I really want the men and women to be able to talk to each other.” Antonin Tron was riffing as we riffled the rails of his first pre-spring collections for Balmain, including this first-ever menswear collection for the house. “I don’t necessarily think of them as a couple,” he qualified: “but I think at Balmain the women are quite sexy and out there. And if I was to do something very different for men, it would be weird.”

Looking at his rails simultaneously, that relationship was manifest in substance as well as spirit: “We have a lot of overcrossing material,” Tron confirmed. Yet there was one fundamental difference in his approach to the two wardrobes: while his Balmain woman is an imagined character, his man is more internalized. “I’m also making things that I can’t find myself,” he said. The tailoring block, for instance, arose from his frustration with suits that were either too narrow or too oversized. Here the shoulders were strong, the waist defined and the fit flattering without constriction: very much surfer’s suiting. “Clothes have to help you, no?”

This approach was also apparent in a bodacious pair of heavy cotton canvas trousers, artfully distressed and cut with articulated knees, whose engineered construction looked more thoughtful than luxury utilitarianism tends to. Alongside them was a boxy, leather-collared canvas jacket whose pockets were edged with metal rivets: work-kink.

Reference-wise Tron’s masculine pantheon ranges from the impishness of 1990s Brad Pitt to the gothic wasted glamour of Lou Reed and Andrew Eldritch, the Sisters of Mercy frontman whose band Tron has seen 25 times. Most significant is David Nuuhiwa, the Hawaiian surfer who disrupted and drove forward that pursuit in California during the 1960s and ’70s. What appeals to Tron, he said, is less the beach aesthetic than Nuuhiwa’s impulse to experiment and push forward.

These influences washed through a wardrobe that veered between faded denim and utilitarian outerwear, leather tailoring, laced trousers, leopard-print silks, and richly embroidered evening pieces (many of whose kaleidoscopically immersive fabrics were, like the denim, shared with the womenswear). The double-breasted suiting was especially powerful, its confidently controlled proportions offering a co-ordinated counterpoint to the more flamboyant material treatments.

Tron sees this sensuality as a rejection of bourgeois convention in luxury: the class-generated current his personal temperament crashes against. His instinct is that luxury should enable freedom rather than impose rules. Entwined within spirit and material, the central filament that connects this new Balmain man and the new Balmain woman is Tron himself. “I think slowly, I’m finding that line: I’m finding the woman and the man, basically.”

Balmain Resort 2027

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Lifestyle  |  Read Source →

“For me, the men’s and the women’s, they are kind of like one entity. I really want the men and women to be able to talk to each other.” Antonin Tron was riffing as we riffled the rails of his first pre-spring collections for Balmain, including his first-ever menswear collection for the house. “I don’t necessarily think of them as a couple,” he qualified: “but I think at Balmain the women are quite sexy and out there. And if I was to do something very different for men, it would be weird.”

Looking at his rails simultaneously, that relationship was manifest in substance as well as spirit: “We have a lot of overcrossing material,” Tron confirmed. The same embroidered surfaces, treated denim, and snakeskin jacquards traveled between the two wardrobes, but in womenswear their expression was differently calibrated. This was a more relaxed continuation of the luxuriously louche and wanton glamour of his last runway collection, which itself had been informed by the Californian desert. “With pre-collection I can explore a bit more the casual side of Balmain,” he said.

That meant injecting a dose of dishevelment into Balmain’s appetite for decoration. Sensuously draped dresses in snakeskin and beautiful, borderline-hallucinogenic florals came with cropped flight jackets, while satin tailoring, sheer lurex blouses, and embellished animalier were countered by washed denim and biker leathers. There were micro cardigans over exposed bras, skirts that succumbed into flowerings of asymmetric ruffle, and lavishly embellished pieces whose surfaces seemed weathered by weeks of sun exposure. What blurred in the haze of Tron’s gaze was much distinction between dressing up and down.

This dissolved tension was more than a styling proposition. Tron has little patience for the idea that luxury dressing must conform either to bourgeois standards of propriety, or fashion’s customary cringe of faux-intellectual snobbishness. “For me, this thing with sexiness is also a class thing,” he said of the physical ideals through which notions of elegance are policed. Balmain, in his view, offers a space to articulate sexuality rather than apologize for it.

He recalled meeting a client in Los Angeles while shooting the collection in Joshua Tree. When he mentioned his last show’s desert-and-hallucinogenics theme, she cheerfully volunteered that she had a house there and used to do exactly that. She was accompanied by her muscular husband. “They both wanted to be hot, and that’s it,” Tron observed. This seemed a pretty good expression of the collection’s carefully direct hedonism. Super-long leather boots and Lucite-heeled wedges provided the strut, while the tasseled Zabriskie bag offered easygoing swing.

His instinct is that luxury should enable freedom rather than impose rules. The filament that connects this new Balmain woman and the new Balmain man, in spirit as much as substance, is Tron himself. “I think slowly, I’m finding that line: I’m finding the woman and the man, basically.”

Bitcoin – How BTC could turn its recent price crash into an $87K rally

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Is Bitcoin’s [BTC] growing negative correlation to other risk assets becoming a key headwind?

It has been less than 48 hours since the most recent crypto wipeout resulted in over $1 billion in liquidations, pushing Bitcoin close to $70k, and sending market sentiment into extreme fear. While Bitcoin continues to trade below $75k, the S&P500 has risen by over 2%, pointing to stronger resilience despite the ongoing FUD.

This growing disparity raises questions about Bitcoin’s performance as a macro hedge this cycle. Notably, Peter Schiff shares his view. As his post below highlights, the long-time Bitcoin critic has warned of a deeper BTC correction, citing its underperformance compared to other risk assets. Even precious metals such as gold and silver have gained over 2%, further widening the performance gap.

Against this backdrop, positioning for further downside is starting to make more sense.

According to CoinGlass data, Bitcoin’s long/short ratio is showing bearish signs as it fell back from a 3-month high of 1.4 on the 7th of November, indicating traders are returning to shorting their positions, signaling weaker confidence in BTC’s near-term recovery. The key takeaway? There is no downside liquidity left to be hunted. The recent $1 billion liquidation event has wiped out almost all long-side liquidity.

This can be considered a great chance for bulls to recover. If Bitcoin remains above $75k and accumulation continues, then both early and late short positions could come under pressure. In fact, one analyst highlighted over $11 billion in short liquidity clustered around $87k.

Interestingly, bulls already seem to be positioning for this potential move.

Despite the scale of the recent wipeout, market sentiment is already showing signs of recovery.

The Crypto Fear & Greed Index shows that sentiment has improved by four points to 32. This indicates that selling pressure may be losing steam and that confidence is returning. Notably, institutional activity is also adding to this shift. As on-chain data reported, MSTR recently bought an additional 1,036 BTC.

To add to this, MARA Holdings has announced a Q3 net profit of $634.4 million, up from $123.1 million in the previous year. This is backed up by Santiment data showing a distinct increase in accumulation between retail and mid-sized holders. In the past two weeks the market has seen an increase of 1-10 BTC wallets by 10,076 BTC and 10-100 BTC wallets by 4,131 BTC.

In short, just because BTC has failed to outperform other assets does not mean that the rally is dying.

Despite macro uncertainty and bearish positioning being a key factor, there are clear signs of increasing institutional exposure and accumulation across wallet classes. If Bitcoin can hold above $75k, the current divergence could turn into a potential bear trap, with the $87k short-liquidity cluster becoming the next major upside target.

Bitcoin is lagging traditional markets, but institutional buying and whale accumulation remain strong.

If Bitcoin holds $75k, a short squeeze could push prices toward $87k.

Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

If you are curious to learn more about Pathways, you can take a look at the following resources:

FWIW, I serve on a couple small endowment investment committees and one started with an advisory. They originally proposed a maddeningly complex investment plan and when we pushed back, they returned with direct investing. My colleagues thought that was a good compromise and I set up a spreadsheet comparing the equity side to VG Total Stock Market. I tax loss aggressively. However, I doubt this would be a good option for you.

I think the best starting position for a taxable account is simply to invest in a low-cost US market index. There are a handful of very good passive index US total market ETFs out there with the primary go-to being VTI. VTI has an expense ratio of 0.03%. Compare that to the 0.4% of the proposed direct indexing fund. If you have $500k in your taxable account, that 0.4% fee is $2,000 per year. Assuming no trading friction costs, compare that to holding a S&P 500 ETF or total US stock ETF with a 0.03% expense ratio cost which would be $150. So you would be spending $1,850 extra per year for this direct indexing tax loss harvesting opportunity.

Can you save over that amount each year in taxes through tax loss harvesting? Maybe, maybe not.

TLH opportunities present themselves most often when you are making regular investments into the taxable account. New purchases are much more likely to provide a TLH opportunity than older positions. In retirement, is your excess income going to result in regular new investments in the taxable account?

TLH can capture losses, but they may not provide much in tax-deductible losses that tax year. Capital losses can off-set capital gains. So if you have realized capital gains you would pay less taxes on them. However, you only have realized gains after you have sold the stock for a gain. if you don't sell any stock, you don't have any gains for that year for the losses to off-set. Eventually you will be able to take those losses as they carry over, but you pay the excess $1,850 in fees every year. You can offset up to $3,000 of ordinary income with capital losses each year. If your marginal rate is 22%, you would save $660 in taxes.

However, keep in mind that the $3,000 off-set is not indexed to inflation. So if you have a $9k capital loss, you will be able to take that over three years. However, the real value of that $9k income deduction decreases with inflation. So the total amount deducted from taxable income in real dollars would be only about $8,783. So the real value of that tax loss would be $660 in year 1, $644 in year 2, and $628 in year 3.

My point isn't the actual numbers but to simply illustrate that the value of the tax loss harvesting they are trying to sell you, may not be very high compared to the management fee.

Colin Farrell's Remake Of A '90s Sci-Fi Movie Is One Of Prime Video's Biggest Streaming Hits

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

"Total Recall" (2012) is directed by Len Wiseman, who most recently oversaw the 2025 "John Wick" offshoot "Ballerina" and previously helmed the "Underworld" films between 2003 and 2016. He is, unfortunately, also responsible for 2007's "Live Free or Die Hard," which, despite making the third spot on /Film's "Die Hard" movie ranking, we simply could have done without.

Unlike the 1990 original, "Total Recall" is set entirely on an Earth ravaged by chemical war, leaving only the United Federation of Britain (UFB) and the Colony (Australia) habitable. Whereas Arnold Schwarzenegger's Douglas Quaid had dreams of Mars, which turn out to be long-buried memories, Colin Farrell's counterpart finds himself experiencing nocturnal visions of being a secret agent. Bored of his factory job on The Colony, he enlists the company Rekall to implant artificial memories to match his dreams, which he soon learns are, in fact, memories of having worked as a UFB agent. Hunted by his former employers, he teams up with Jessica Biel's Melina, a member of a rebel group known as The Resistance. But as the pair try to outrun the UFB and their Chancellor Vilos Cohaagen (Bryan Cranston), the line between fantasy and reality becomes increasingly blurred.

Ahead of the film's debut, /Film asked: "Is 'Total Recall' the least essential film of the summer?" The answer, according to most critics, was yes. But it seems the Prime Video crowds are willing to give it another shot. Per streaming viewership tracker FlixPatrol, "Total Recall" is the No. 3 most-watched movie on Prime Video in the United States as of November 9, 2026. What's more, based on the film's trajectory thus far, it could be set for a chart-topping run. "Total Recall" debuted on the charts at No. 9 on November 7, 2026, before jumping to fourth the following day and hitting No. 3 the day after. By the end of the week, Farrell's forgotten remake might just hit No. 1 — not bad for a film with a 30% critic score on Rotten Tomatoes.

Both Philip K. Dick's story and the 1990 "Total Recall" film feature Mars. But the 2012 remake actually has more in common with Paul Verhoeven's rendition than the novel, using characters that only appear in the movie and adhering more closely to the big-screen storyline. That might have seemed like a reasonable approach, given how the first film was so successful. Unfortunately, Len Wiseman's effort didn't emulate the offbeat comedy of the original, which was so sharply rendered by Arnold Schwarzenegger and gave the film such a unique tone. Despite featuring some impressive action scenes, Wiseman's version also managed to feel like a generic sci-fi actioner, all of which combined to yield a less-than-impressive critical response.

That said, Roger Ebert was impressed enough to bestow three stars on the film — just half a star less than the 1990 version — and called it a "well-crafted, high-energy sci-fi." Still, even he had to admit that the film was one of several sci-fi movie remakes that should have never happened, ending his review by writing, "It never touched me emotionally, though, the way the 1990 film did, and strictly speaking, isn't necessary." Others were much more condemnatory, with David Sexton of the London Evening Standard giving the film one star and writing, "We used to do the future so much better. Let's not forget that. Let's forget this instead."

Normally, that should deter you from joining the "Total Recall" renaissance on Prime Video. But considering that, since the movie's debut, we've been subjected to an onslaught of streaming movie shabbiness, it might be worth a go to see if the remake really is bad or whether that's merely a memory implanted by the critics.

Jarvis Osorio: When Three AI Agents Agree—and All Three Are Wrong

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I am Jarvis Osorio (Jarvis Nelson Osorio), a technology entrepreneur and innovator working on autonomous AI agents, cloud-agent runtimes and institutional design. One of the most dangerous feelings in a multi-agent demo is relief: several agents independently "agree," so the answer must be solid.

I first sketched this trap in a LinkedIn note—Three AI agents agreed on the answer. All three were wrong.—and it keeps showing up in production pipelines.

When agents share the same model family, the same prompt template or the same retrieved context, they do not double-check each other. They echo each other. Agreement becomes a costume for correlated error. The mistake ships with a chorus behind it.

This is the multi-agent version of a yes-machine. In The Compliant Mind I describe how systems trained on approval drift toward pleasant certainty. Multi-agent stacks can manufacture that certainty socially—agent to agent—without ever touching a fact.

Three practices keep showing their value:

Those habits are institutional design applied to inference. They match the DAO lessons in Breaking the Centralized Order: separate proposer from approver, write limits into the system, leave a trail.

A research agent, a drafting agent and a "critic" agent all endorse the same citation. The citation looks academic. None of them fetched the page. The critic used the same retrieved snippets as the others, so its dissent was cosmetic. The article published. Readers found the source did not exist. Three green votes, one broken trust.

The fix was boring and effective: the critic switched model families; a fetch step had to return HTTP 200 and a title match before the citation could leave draft; the verdict packet was stored where the agents could not overwrite it. Disagreement returned—and that was the success.

If your architecture diagram shows three identical workers voting, you have built a choir, not a court. Diversity of model, tool access and evaluation criteria is not academic purity—it is how you catch confident nonsense before customers do.

Also resist the urge to "fix" disagreement by averaging prose. Disagreement is signal. Collapse it only after an external check, not because a dashboard prefers a green checkmark.

Before an agent output leaves your boundary, ask:

Agents that can act need the same seriousness we demand from payment systems and governance modules. Speed without verification is just a faster way to be wrong together.

Agreement feels like safety. Independence plus evidence is closer to the real thing.

Base64 Overhead: Why Data URIs Make Files 33% Bigger

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 10 • Originally published at toolstead.net

Base64 makes data bigger. Not slightly bigger — a third bigger, every time, with no exceptions and no settings to tune. The number is not a quirk of any particular implementation; it falls directly out of the encoding, and understanding where it comes from makes the inlining decision much easier.

Base64 exists to represent arbitrary bytes using only characters that survive text transport. The alphabet has 64 symbols, which is 6 bits of information per character. Bytes are 8 bits. So the encoder reads 3 bytes (24 bits) at a time and writes 4 characters (4 × 6 = 24 bits).

That is the whole story. A 300,000-byte image becomes a 400,000-character string. The overhead is exactly 33.3%, and it applies to the payload only — the data:image/png;base64, prefix adds a further 22 characters, which matters for tiny assets and is irrelevant for large ones.

The one escape hatch is padding. If the input length is not a multiple of three, the last group is padded with = characters that carry no information, so the exact size is 4 × ceil(n / 3):

The small-input entries are the argument against inlining tiny assets for size reasons: a 1-byte file becomes 4 characters plus a 22-character prefix, and the prefix dominates.

On text, gzip routinely achieves 60–80% reduction, which is why the 33% figure is sometimes dismissed as irrelevant. That reasoning holds for JSON and HTML and fails completely for images.

Compression works by finding redundancy. JPEG, PNG, WebP and AVIF have already removed almost all of it — that is what those formats do — so their bytes are close to random from a compressor's point of view. Base64 encodes those incompressible bytes into a restricted 64-symbol alphabet, which adds no redundancy a compressor can exploit. The result is the worst of both worlds: the data is 33% larger and still does not compress.

The garbled case is different. If you Base64-encode a text payload — JSON, XML, a log — gzip will compress the encoded form well, sometimes better than the original, because Base64 of text still contains recognisable structure. So "does gzip fix the 33%" has two answers, and they depend entirely on whether the payload was compressible to begin with.

Inflating a stylesheet or an HTML document has three costs that do not show up in a file-size comparison:

None of these are fatal. All of them are invisible if you only compare file sizes.

Two of those three costs come from how the string is embedded rather than from Base64 itself, which is worth separating: what a data URI actually is walks the grammar and the four ways one fails to render, and most of them are wrapping problems rather than encoding problems.

There is no universal threshold, but the decision is narrow enough to state as rules of thumb:

For images specifically, there is a better question than "how big is the Base64 string": could this be an SVG? A vector logo is a few hundred bytes of path data, encodes to under a kilobyte, and is resolution-independent. Inlining that is a clear win, and it is the case where the technique earns its reputation.

The table above covers round numbers. For a real file, convert an image and watch the payload grow — the page reports the exact character count, the byte count and the overhead that falls out of the two, in the Data URI, plain and CSS forms.

Two numbers tell you whether inlining is helping: the transfer size of the document that contains the data URI, and the transfer size of the equivalent page with external assets. If the inlined version is 30% larger, it is 30% larger — the 33% is not recoverable by the network layer.

The measurement is easy to get wrong in one specific way: comparing the uncompressed size of the image with the compressed size of the document. Compare like with like, and remember that the data URI's bytes travel inside a response that gzip can only partially shrink. Our PNG to Base64 and JPG to Base64 pages report the output length and the overhead as you convert, which is usually enough to see immediately that a 900 KB photograph should not be an inline background image.

For the cases where you do want the string — a small icon in critical CSS, an email template that cannot reference external files, a single-file HTML export, a JSON fixture in a test — the overhead is a fair price for self-containment, and knowing the exact number keeps it a deliberate choice rather than an accident. If you are deciding between the two ways of holding an image in memory rather than in a document, data URI vs blob URL covers the other half of this trade.

Alzheimer’s may have early warning signs outside the brain

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Alzheimer's disease is best known for its effects on memory and thinking, but new research suggests that some symptoms may develop in a surprising place: outside the brain.

Scientists at the University of Central Florida (UCF) have discovered that genetic mutations associated with Alzheimer's can interfere with the nerves that control muscle movement, even without involvement from the brain or spinal cord. The findings could help explain why some people with Alzheimer's experience changes in walking, balance, or coordination long before noticeable memory problems emerge.

The discovery raises the possibility that movement difficulties could provide earlier warning signs of the disease and that treatments focused exclusively on the brain may overlook important problems elsewhere in the body.

The research was led by James Hickman, a professor at UCF's Nanoscience Technology Center, and Research Professor Xiufang "Nadine" Guo, working alongside scientists at the healthcare technology company Hesperos. Funded by the National Institutes of Health's National Institute on Aging, the study used laboratory systems built from human cells to investigate how inherited Alzheimer's mutations affect movement. The findings were published in Alzheimer's & Dementia: The Journal of the Alzheimer's Association.

"Motor deficits may be an earlier indication [of Alzheimer's]," Guo says. "If we can detect those changes and intervene earlier, that could help delay the onset of central nervous system symptoms."

Why Alzheimer's Symptoms May Appear Before Memory Loss

Alzheimer's is generally associated with progressive memory loss, confusion, and declining cognitive abilities. However, doctors have also observed that some patients develop physical symptoms, including changes in balance, coordination, and gait (manner of walking), years before clear cognitive difficulties become apparent.

These observations have raised an important question: Are such movement problems simply a consequence of damage occurring in the brain, or could Alzheimer's also interfere directly with nerves elsewhere in the body?

To investigate, the UCF team focused on familial Alzheimer's, a rare inherited form of the disease that typically develops earlier in life (from 40 to 65 years of age) than the more common form.

Their experiments revealed that motor neurons carrying mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's could disrupt the connections responsible for controlling muscles, even when the brain and spinal cord were completely absent from the experimental system.

Motor neurons are specialized nerve cells that transmit signals to muscles, telling them when to contract and produce movement. The new findings suggest that Alzheimer's mutations can interfere with this process directly, rather than affecting movement only through damage to the brain.

"This is the first time it's been demonstrated that deficits in the peripheral nervous system can arise directly from these mutations," Hickman says. "It means drugs that target the brain may not fix problems in the rest of the body."

The peripheral nervous system includes the nerves located outside the brain and spinal cord. These nerves carry signals between the central nervous system and the rest of the body, helping control movement, sensation, and other essential functions.

Guo also points out that preserving the ability to move could have benefits beyond physical health. Regular physical activity is associated with better cognitive well-being, so protecting motor function may also help support brain health.

How a Miniature Human Cell System Revealed the Discovery

To examine the relationship between Alzheimer's mutations and muscle movement, the scientists used an innovative laboratory platform known as "human-on-a-chip" technology.

Developed and manufactured through Hesperos, a company co-founded by Hickman, these miniature systems bring different types of human cells together under controlled conditions. They allow researchers to recreate specific biological functions and observe how diseases interfere with them.

Unlike conventional laboratory experiments that examine isolated cells, these systems can reproduce important interactions between different tissues. They also provide an alternative to animal models, which do not always accurately reflect how human diseases behave.

For this study, the team created a miniature model of the neuromuscular junction, the connection where a motor neuron communicates with a muscle cell.

The model was deliberately designed without a brain or spinal cord. This was a crucial part of the experiment because it allowed the researchers to investigate whether Alzheimer's-related changes could cause movement problems independently of the central nervous system.

The scientists combined healthy muscle cells with motor neurons derived from stem cells that carried genetic mutations associated with familial Alzheimer's disease. Stem cells are cells that can develop into specialized types of cells, making them valuable tools for studying human diseases in the laboratory.

The researchers then examined how effectively the altered nerve cells communicated with otherwise healthy muscle tissue.

The results showed that the Alzheimer's-associated mutations could interfere with normal nerve-to-muscle function. This suggests that at least some movement difficulties linked to the disease may arise directly in the peripheral nervous system rather than being caused entirely by degeneration in the brain.

Why Damaged Nerve Connections Can Affect Walking and Balance

Every voluntary movement depends on successful communication between the nervous system and the muscles.

At the neuromuscular junction, an electrical signal traveling along a motor neuron triggers chemical communication that causes a muscle to contract. This process makes everyday activities possible, from walking and maintaining posture to lifting objects and performing precise movements.

When the connection becomes impaired, muscles may respond less effectively to nerve signals. Depending on the severity and location of the problem, this can contribute to weakness, reduced coordination, and difficulty sustaining physical activity.

To evaluate these effects, the research team measured how consistently nerve signals produced muscle contractions and how long the muscles could continue contracting before becoming fatigued.

These measurements provided a direct assessment of how well the nerve and muscle cells worked together. They also resemble aspects of the functional testing physicians use when examining patients with movement disorders.

"You can't move unless the motor circuit works," Hickman says. "When a doctor taps your knee to check your reflex, they're testing that exact connection."

By demonstrating impaired nerve-to-muscle communication in a system containing no brain or spinal cord, the study provides evidence that mutations linked to familial Alzheimer's can produce effects beyond the central nervous system.

However, the experiments do not establish that these changes necessarily occur before memory loss in patients. Further research will be needed to determine how closely the laboratory findings reflect the progression of Alzheimer's in people, particularly those with the more common forms of the disease.

Could This Discovery Lead to Earlier Alzheimer's Detection?

The findings suggest a potentially important new direction for Alzheimer's research: looking beyond memory and other cognitive symptoms when investigating the disease.

If future studies confirm that changes in motor function are detectable early in certain patients, tests of walking, coordination, or nerve-to-muscle activity might eventually help identify disease-related changes before more obvious neurological symptoms develop.

The results also raise questions about treatment. Many Alzheimer's therapies are designed to address processes occurring in the brain, but medications that target the central nervous system may not necessarily correct abnormalities affecting peripheral nerves and muscles.

Understanding these separate effects could help researchers investigate treatments that protect physical function alongside cognitive health.

Human-on-a-Chip Technology Could Transform Disease Research

Beyond the implications for Alzheimer's, the researchers see considerable potential in using human-on-a-chip systems to investigate other diseases and evaluate possible treatments.

Because these platforms contain human cells and reproduce measurable biological activities, they can help scientists observe disease-related changes in conditions that more closely resemble human physiology than some conventional experimental models.

This approach may be particularly useful in drug development, where researchers need to understand not only how a potential treatment affects individual cells but also how it influences interactions between different tissues.

Such systems can also uncover biological effects that may be difficult to detect in animal experiments, potentially helping researchers identify promising therapies and recognize limitations earlier in development.

For Hickman, the progress represents the culmination of three decades of work devoted to understanding disease through more realistic laboratory models.

"These systems let us study disease in a way that's closer to what actually happens in the human body, and that's what we need to develop better treatments," he says.

Materials provided by University of Central Florida. Original written by Emily Dougherty. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

Planning a trip with your family inside one chat: Wabi's multiplayer threads, explained

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I'm the author of Wabi Wiki, an unofficial guide to the Wabi AI assistant — not affiliated with Wabi.

Most group trips don't fail because nobody cares. They fail because the plan lives in five places at once: one person screenshots flight prices, another drops an Airbnb link, a third asks "wait, which dates are we doing again?" three days later. By departure day, nobody is quite sure what's booked.

Wabi — the free personal AI assistant from Replika founder Eugenia Kuyda, publicly launched on October 19, 2026 — tries a different shape. Instead of everyone running a private chat with their own assistant, you share a thread with your partner, your family, or your friends, and everyone in it talks to the same assistant and uses the same tools. The company calls this "multiplayer," and it's the feature Kuyda talks about most.

Here's what that looks like for a family trip.

You open one shared thread and say you're planning a long weekend away. Wabi can build the planning tools right inside the conversation — no app store, no separate download. Ask for an itinerary and it builds a small itinerary app in the thread. Need a decision? Ask for a poll on dates or destinations and everyone votes where they already are. A budget tracker keeps the running total visible to the whole group, a shared packing list stops the "did anyone bring the charger?" thread, and a countdown can sit on your home screen as a widget so the trip stays visible without opening the app.

The part that makes it different from a shared doc is that the assistant is in the room. In Wabi 2.0 the agent doesn't only build what you ask for — it also decides on its own which apps to create, starting from the moment you connect your email. And because the thread is shared, the questions Wabi asks get answered by whoever knows: your partner confirms the hotel dates, your sister picks the restaurant, and the answers land in the same place.

Three things worth knowing before you try it.

First, Wabi is English-first and runs on iOS, Android and Mac. There is no Windows app and no web version, so if part of the group lives on a PC, they'll be joining from their phone.

Second, privacy is scoped per chat. A family thread doesn't automatically see what your finance thread sees, and people in a chat can see when personal information is requested inside it. Wabi also says it checks with you before important actions that need your approval — worth remembering before you let it handle a booking or a refund.

Third, Wabi is free for now and the company hasn't settled on a business model. That's a good reason to try it, and a good reason not to build a critical workflow on it yet.

The honest test is a small one: pick one real plan — a weekend away, a birthday dinner, a household chore rota — put it in one shared thread, and see whether the group actually stays in that thread instead of drifting back to screenshots. If it works, the mini-apps are doing their job. If it doesn't, you've lost an evening, not a system.

If you want the full picture — threads, mini-apps, widgets, errands, and the health and connections features — the full features guide on Wabi Wiki walks through each one in plain language.

Wabi Wiki is an unofficial guide to the Wabi AI assistant, not affiliated with Wabi.

Jarvis Nelson Osorio: Why Lightweight AI Agent Runtimes Matter

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I am Jarvis Nelson Osorio (Jarvis Osorio)—tech entrepreneur, AI innovator, systems architect, DAO designer, author and artist. The agent story that dominated early demos was a heavy personal assistant: big install, big memory footprint, broad permissions, and a chat window that felt magical until you asked what it could touch on your machine.

I originally explored a sharper version of this debate on LinkedIn in Why ZeroClaw is the "OpenClaw Killer". Names of runtimes will keep changing. The design question will not: should an agent be a bloated application, or a small, sandboxed kernel with explicit authority?

Large agent frameworks popularized the dream of a capable personal agent. They also normalized:

Those choices are fine for a lab demo. They are poor defaults for production agents that sit next to email, code, money or family data.

A serious edge-oriented agent runtime aims for a different profile:

Efficiency is not only about milliseconds. It is about where the agent can live and how hard it is to accidentally grant it the keys to everything.

The industry shift I care about is transactional authority: agents that settle a step, file a ticket, or call an API—not only draft a paragraph. The moment an agent can do, you need institutions around it. In Breaking the Centralized Order I argue that roles, separation of powers, written limits and an audit trail are not bureaucracy; they are how systems survive contact with reality.

A tiny, sandboxed runtime makes those controls easier to enforce than a kitchen-sink desktop process that already owns your shell.

When your agent must fit in a few megabytes of RAM, you stop shipping kitchen-sink plugins "just in case." You choose tools. You write allowlists. You decide which memories deserve vector storage and which are noise. Those constraints produce clearer products than unlimited cloud quotas, which invite sprawl.

The same discipline shows up in DAO design: scarcity of permissions forces clarity of roles. Lightweight runtimes are scarcity applied to agent engineering—and scarcity, used well, is a feature.

Before you fall in love with a feature list, ask four questions:

If the answers are vague, you do not have a personal agent. You have a remote control with optimism.

More writing and profiles: jarvisosorio.com · article hub. For the human cost of systems that never refuse, see The Compliant Mind.

Small, strict and portable beats large, loose and impressive—especially on the day something goes wrong.

Destination Spotlight: Crystal Mountain

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Skiing at Crystal Mountain, Michigan. (Photo: Crystal Mountain)

As a proud Michigander, I spent many childhood summers “Up North,” a region of the state with beautiful lakes, cozy cabins, extensive hiking and biking trails, and every other manner of outdoor summer fun. But “Up North” isn’t strictly a summer destination; it’s a four-season adventure hub that offers everything from spectacular fall leaf-peeping to world-class winter skiing.

Among the best of these Michigan winter playgrounds is Crystal Mountain. Just a short drive from Traverse City and a stone’s throw from Sleeping Bear Dunes National Lakeshore, Crystal Mountain got its start 70 years ago with a single rope tow, an old car engine, and a dream. From these humble beginnings, it has blossomed into one of the best and most visited ski hills in the state, with 59 downhill runs, six chairlifts, two magic carpets, and more than 100 acres of skiable terrain.

The mission at the heart of the operation has always remained the same: to be a place where families come together to have fun, make memories, and experience all the best the outdoors have to offer. This philosophy has immortalized Crystal Mountain in the hearts of midwesterners like me, encouraging generation after generation of skiers to fall in love with the sport and the outdoors. It’s where I learned to ski—and where you can still take lessons from PSIA/AASI-trained instructors.

Beyond the ski hill, there’s no shortage of things to do at Crystal. Glide through this winter wonderland on cross-country skis—the resort’s 25 kilometers of trails were named best in the state by Midwest Living Magazine. Or let a team of Belgian horses do the gliding for you as you enjoy the views of the forests and hills from a horse-drawn carriage. Lace up your ice skates in the heart of the resort’s village at Barr Park, or explore the Michigan Legacy Art Park, a 30-acre reserve located on the grounds of Crystal Mountain where you can snowshoe, hike, and take in the sculptures and scenery.

When you’ve had enough of the cold, unwind at the outdoor hot tub, the indoor pool at the Peak Fitness Center, or the award-winning Crystal Spa. Consistently recognized as one of the best in the country, Crystal Spa features infrared saunas, eucalyptus steam rooms, and a relaxation lounge with a cozy fireplace, making it the perfect oasis after a day spent out in the winter weather.

There are endless ways to enjoy Crystal Mountain, but the true magic of this place can’t be credited to any one thing—it’s the feeling you get when you’re here. It’s the memories you’ll cherish for a lifetime.

Established in 1956, Crystal Mountain is a family-owned, four-season resort located 28 miles southwest of Traverse City, Michigan, and a short drive from Sleeping Bear Dunes National Lakeshore. The 1,500-acre resort features lodging, dining, downhill and cross-country skiing, two championship golf courses, shopping, the award-winning Crystal Spa, Michigan’s only alpine slide, Peak Fitness, the Park at Water’s Edge, Michigan Legacy Art Park, weddings, a conference center, and real estate options.
