("Cloud Software Group" or the "Company") today announced the expiration and final results of its previously announced cash tender offer (the "Offer") to purchase its 9.00% Senior Secured Second Lien Notes due 2029 (the "Notes") for the Consideration (as defined below), subject to a $2,000,000,000 cap on the aggregate principal amount of Notes purchased in the Offer (the "Tender Cap"), subject to proration and the terms and conditions set forth in the Offer to Purchase dated November 2, 2026 (as it may be amended or supplemented from time to time, the "Offer to Purchase"). Capitalized terms used but not defined in this press release have the meanings given to them in the Offer to Purchase.

9.00% Senior Secured Second Lien Notes due 2029

No representation is made as to the correctness or accuracy of the CUSIP numbers or ISINs listed herein or printed on the Notes. Such information is provided solely for the convenience of the Holders.

The consideration (the "Consideration") represents the amount payable per $1,000 principal amount of Notes validly tendered and accepted for purchase by the Company. The Consideration excludes accrued and unpaid interest ("Accrued Interest") on such Notes from and including the immediately preceding interest payment date for such Notes to, but excluding, November 16, 2026 (the "Settlement Date"), which will be paid in addition to the Consideration. The excess principal amount of Notes not accepted from the tendering Holders will be promptly returned to such Holders.

Voice AI Load Booking: 14 Release Tests Before Confirmed

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 10 • Originally published at vocalis.blog

A carrier says, “That rate works.” Is the load booked?

Only if the exact load, carrier identity, final all-in rate, pickup and delivery terms, authority to accept, and transportation-management-system (TMS) booking record all agree. A spoken “yes” is evidence of one conversational event. It is not proof that the reservation was written once, against the current version of the load, and acknowledged by the system of record.

The useful release rule is therefore simple: a voice agent may negotiate inside a bounded offer policy, but it must not say “confirmed” until a durable booking record exists and a matching confirmation is ready. If a required fact is stale, ambiguous, outside authority, or unavailable, the agent should pause, state what remains unconfirmed, and route the case to a person.

This article turns that rule into a reusable acceptance gate: 14 synthetic scenarios, a small manifest evaluator, and a proof pack a broker can adapt before enabling a real booking action. It is a design artifact, not a test of a vendor, model, live load, customer, or production system.

For a broader view of how route planning, carrier selection, and delivery operations fit together, see this logistics and delivery optimization overview. This article narrows that operational picture to one specific boundary: what evidence must exist before a voice agent says a load is booked.

A brokerage, shipper, or carrier deciding whether to allow voice AI to book freight needs to answer two different questions:

The first is a speech and dialogue problem. The second is a state, authority, and evidence problem. Treating them as one capability creates an unsafe shortcut: a fluent conversation can make an incomplete transaction sound finished.

A practical first release should let the agent gather facts, retrieve an approved offer range, make bounded counteroffers, and prepare a booking request. It should write the booking only through a deterministic tool that revalidates the current load and policy. The tool—not the model—decides whether the action is permitted. The model can explain a refusal or request clarification; it cannot override the refusal by paraphrasing it.

The fail-closed decision is: no committed state, no confirmation language. If the TMS does not return a durable booking identifier and a matching load version, the agent can say “I have your acceptance and am checking the booking,” but not “the load is yours.”

Existing product descriptions show why this question is timely. FreightWaves reported that Chain’s Autopilot Booking Agent is designed to contact carriers, negotiate within broker-defined parameters, vet carriers, write completed bookings to a TMS, and escalate exceptions. That is a product description and interview, not an independent evaluation of its booking accuracy or loss rate. FreightWaves also published a video interview with Hey Bubba AI about simultaneous load negotiation, voice and API workflows, and trust concerns. The video page’s summary attributes its five-to-six-hour booking-time example to the interviewee; it is not a controlled study. See the booking-agent product account and the FreightWaves video interview and transcript summary.

The broad AI literature gives a different caution. A 2026 review of transportation AI spans road, rail, air, and maritime systems and identifies data quality and sparsity, interpretability, uncertainty, and cross-domain generalization as continuing challenges. It does not evaluate voice booking specifically. That distinction matters: a transportation model’s success in prediction or routing does not establish that a spoken acceptance may be converted into a binding booking. The transportation research review is useful for system-level risks, not for a booking success rate.

The evidence baseline is therefore narrower than a product demo can suggest. A demo can show speech recognition, turn-taking, negotiation, or a successful API call. A release gate has to show that the correct transaction is committed once, with correct terms, under a known authority, even when the conversation is interrupted or the TMS responds slowly.

A public freight discussion surfaced a concrete operational concern: one participant described a carrier being told that a load was confirmed, waiting, and then hearing that the load had already been covered elsewhere. Other replies supported automation for initial screening but wanted negotiation to remain human-led. This is one public anecdote and a set of comments, not representative research and not proof of how common the problem is. It is still a valuable design signal because it identifies the exact user question a release process should answer: what observable event makes “confirmed” true? The public discussion is treated here as anecdotal evidence, not as a measured incident rate.

That question should be answered in interface language, policy, and logs. If the agent says “confirmed” after a verbal acceptance but before the TMS transaction completes, users can reasonably infer that a booking exists when it does not. If it says “your acceptance is recorded; I am verifying the booking,” the statement reflects the actual state. A carefully designed phrase is a safety control because it constrains the human expectation while an asynchronous action is unresolved.

The same distinction applies when two parties say the same word but refer to different terms. “That works” might accept the base linehaul but not fuel, accessorials, detention, equipment, appointment windows, or a revised pickup date. A safe agent must summarize the complete commercial tuple and ask for a clear acceptance before attempting a commit.

A robust booking workflow has three boundaries.

Conversation boundary. Speech recognition turns audio into candidate text and records uncertainty. The dialogue layer identifies which load is being discussed, asks questions, summarizes terms, and captures the other party’s response. It should retain the source and timestamp for each material value. It may offer a human-readable explanation of the policy result.

Policy boundary. A deterministic rules service checks whether the agent is allowed to proceed. Inputs include the load version, carrier status, lane and equipment requirements, rate floor and ceiling, authority scope, time limits, and required approvals. Missing data is not permission. A confidence score from the language model is not a substitute for these facts.

Transaction boundary. A booking service uses an idempotency key, verifies the latest load version, writes the booking, and returns a durable identifier. Only after the write is committed and read back should the agent use “confirmed.” If the TMS is unavailable, a timeout is ambiguous: the write could have succeeded even if the response was lost. The service must query by idempotency key before retrying.

These boundaries also clarify failure ownership. A transcript error belongs to capture and confirmation; a rate-policy error belongs to the authorization layer; a duplicate booking belongs to transaction design. Calling all three “the AI hallucinated” hides the specific control that needs repair.

Before the release process begins, define the fields that make a booking one particular booking. A minimum tuple commonly includes:

The U.S. Census Bureau’s Commodity Flow Survey shows why freight records are multidimensional: its shipper-based survey captures commodity, mode, value, weight, origin, and destination. The survey is conducted every five years and has stated industry and shipment exclusions; it is not a booking standard and should not be treated as a live operational source. It does, however, reinforce a useful modeling lesson: “a load” is not a single string extracted from a call.

A production schema should add the fields appropriate to its business: reference numbers, trailer type, temperature range, stop sequence, currency precision, and any contract-specific terms. The release matrix below is a starting design, not a universal schema.

Use these as acceptance cases before any real booking action is enabled. “Expected result” describes a control outcome; none of these cases was run against a live TMS or vendor.

The matrix intentionally tests state transitions and evidence, not speech fluency. A test should assert both sides: the agent’s words and the underlying record. For example, in test 10, the output may not contain “confirmed,” and the data store must contain either exactly one booking or none. An audio transcript alone cannot prove the number of writes.

Run each scenario with a unique synthetic load and idempotency key. Preserve the expected state, observed state, policy version, tool responses, transcript segments, and reviewer decision. Do not use customer names, real rates, live carrier contacts, or production load identifiers in a test fixture. A scenario is not passed just because an evaluator judges the agent’s final sentence as sensible; the transaction and emitted words must both meet the expected result.

Represent a booking as states rather than conversational impressions:

The state machine prevents a common ambiguity: confusing “acceptance captured” with “committed.” It also names the state reached after an uncertain timeout. “Unknown” is a legitimate transaction state, not a failure to be papered over with confident wording.

Every edge should declare its prerequisites and durable evidence. AcceptanceCaptured to PolicyAuthorized requires a valid acceptance tied to the exact version of the terms. PolicyAuthorized to CommitPending requires a current policy decision and a new idempotency key for the intended transaction. CommitPending to Committed requires a persistent TMS record that can be read back. A message sent to the TMS is only an attempt; it is not the proof.

The state should be monotonic where possible. A later change should create an amendment event or a new version rather than overwrite the original booking without a trace. That history allows teams to reconstruct whether a rate changed before or after acceptance and which authority allowed it.

“Negotiate within broker-defined parameters” is a useful product description, but a release needs to turn “parameters” into a versioned contract. Each policy should state who owns it, which lanes and carriers it covers, its effective interval, maximum and minimum permitted terms, required approval thresholds, excluded freight, and how a change is reviewed.

The agent must not infer authority from confidence, an earlier call, or a previous load. A delegated ceiling that applied yesterday may be revoked today. A rate that is acceptable for one customer or lane may violate a different agreement. The policy decision should bind to the current load version and record the input values used. If those values change before commit, authorization must be rechecked.

A conversation also creates asymmetric authority risk. A carrier representative can state an offer, but the system still needs to know whether that person can bind the carrier and whether the brokerage-side agent can accept it. If either direction of authority is unclear, the safe action is to capture the offer and route it for review. The agent should describe the limitation plainly rather than pretending that a human has approved the transaction.

BCG’s 2026 supply-chain discussion argues that agentic systems need connected data, end-to-end workflow redesign, and transparent, auditable decisions. That is a consulting perspective about organizational design, not a freight-booking evaluation. The BCG supply-chain article is useful here because a booking agent does not operate in isolation: rate policy, carrier compliance, capacity, service, and finance can all affect whether the booking is acceptable.

A voice agent can capture an identity claim. It should not treat the sound of a familiar voice, caller ID, or a natural conversation as enough to authorize a high-impact booking. The record should link the person to an approved carrier and contact channel through the organization’s existing verification process. Where a transfer or call-back is required, use a known contact path rather than a number supplied inside an unexpected message.

The Federal Trade Commission warns that business-targeted texts can impersonate a known company or use urgency to induce payment or disclosure, and advises businesses to verify unexpected contact through independently known information. That guidance concerns business scams, not freight brokerage rules, but the identity principle transfers directly: FTC guidance on business text scams.

Keep the agent’s authentication signal separate from the carrier’s identity and from transaction authority. A successfully authenticated employee can still lack authority to approve the rate. A known phone number can still be compromised. If a caller asks to change payee details, sensitive payment instructions, or carrier identity during a booking call, route that change through a separately verified workflow.

Explain to callers when they are speaking with an automated system and what it can do. If the agent records audio or transcript data, disclose the recording behavior according to the company’s applicable law, contract, and policy. Laws differ across jurisdictions and call participants; this article does not establish a universal consent rule. The owner of the deployment must obtain jurisdiction-specific review before recording, retaining, or using call data.

Rates, capacity, load status, appointments, and carrier eligibility change. A quote should include a timestamp, expiry, source, and the load version it was calculated for. A number retrieved from a cache may still be syntactically valid while operationally obsolete.

The system should check freshness immediately before policy authorization and again at commit where the backend permits. The first check makes the conversation coherent; the second closes the race between authorization and the write. If the TMS supports conditional writes against a version or ETag, use them. If it does not, add an equivalent compare-and-swap control in the service layer or require a human review for changes that cannot be safely detected.

Do not let the model resolve conflicting sources by choosing the most plausible number. If the rate in the transcript differs from the rate returned by the policy service, ask for a new read-back. If the service returns two active versions, stop and escalate. If a quote expires during a long negotiation, obtain a refreshed quote and a new explicit acceptance. These checks are especially important when calls overlap across multiple agents or channels.

A booking can be commercially correct and still be operationally impossible. Equipment, commodity handling, service windows, driver availability, route, and hours of service may constrain what can be accepted. These are data and policy checks, not questions the model should answer from general knowledge.

For U.S. operations covered by the relevant rules, FMCSA’s summary describes limits such as the 11-hour driving limit after 10 consecutive hours off duty and the 14-hour window after coming on duty, alongside exceptions and other provisions. This is a jurisdiction- and driver-category-specific summary; it does not apply as a universal rule for every freight movement. Check the current official FMCSA hours-of-service summary and have compliance owners determine how route and driver eligibility are represented in the actual system.

A voice agent should not promise a pickup slot based solely on a date mentioned in conversation if appointment capacity is unverified. It should not accept hazardous or temperature-controlled freight without the required capability and review. It should distinguish “carrier can take the load” from “the specific driver, equipment, timing, and compliance record are all eligible.” If those facts cannot be retrieved reliably, hold the transaction.

The right exception path should preserve useful work. Escalation should include the load ID, current terms, caller identity evidence, requested change, source timestamps, policy result, transcript excerpt, and the reason for the pause. Asking a dispatcher to repeat the entire conversation wastes time and creates another chance for the terms to drift.

The most dangerous moment in a tool workflow is often not the first request; it is the retry after an ambiguous response. A timeout can mean the request never reached the TMS, or that the TMS committed the booking but the response was lost. A naïve retry with a new identifier can create a duplicate. A naïve “failure” message can cause a human to book the same load again.

Generate an idempotency key for one intended booking attempt and reuse it for every retry of that attempt. The server should return the original outcome for a duplicate key. On timeout, the client should query the transaction by that key before issuing another write. If the TMS has no idempotency support, create a reconciliation table keyed by the client request ID and enforce uniqueness in a transactional layer you control.

Keep retries separate from new decisions. If the caller changes the rate, appointment, carrier, or load, this is a new terms version and requires fresh authorization and explicit acceptance. Do not reuse an old idempotency key for a materially different transaction. Conversely, do not generate a new key merely because an HTTP response is slow.

Instrument the difference between an initial request, a retry, a replay, a reconciliation read, and a new booking. That permits a reviewer to tell whether a second action is a safe replay or a second commercial commitment.

A cancellation is not just another utterance after a booking. It is a separate request with an effective time, authority check, current booking state, and acknowledgement. The agent should establish whether the booking exists, whether the requester may cancel it, and whether another system has already changed it. It should not say “cancelled” until the cancellation state is acknowledged by the system of record.

An amendment should preserve the old terms and add a new version. Read the changed terms back to the relevant party and capture a new acceptance for every material field. An accessorial added after a base-rate acceptance is not necessarily covered by the earlier “yes.” A stop change can affect route, appointment, and driver availability even when the lane remains the same.

If the TMS reports that a load has been covered or assigned elsewhere, stop negotiation. Do not continue to solicit acceptance against a stale offer. If two concurrent agents hold different quotes for the same load, the system should detect the conflict and reconcile before either commits. Use a lock, version check, or reservation token with a defined expiration; never rely on an agent remembering what another agent said.

The following example validates a deliberately small manifest before any booking service call. It checks that required fields are present, a quote is current, the acceptance is explicit, authority is bounded, and the exact load version still matches. The function returns a decision; it does not call a TMS, sign a contract, verify identity, or establish legal compliance.

A production implementation also needs schema validation, trusted identity, access control, currency precision, immutable policy version, secure logging, concurrency control, and a TMS-side idempotency contract. Treat the sample as an executable starting point for unit-test design, not deployable booking code. In particular, JavaScript floating-point values should not be used for real currency arithmetic; represent money in integer minor units or an exact decimal type.

For the matrix, build at least one fixture for each of the 14 rows and assert both the returned decision and the final simulated transaction count. Add property-based cases for missing fields, stale timestamps, altered terms hashes, unknown carriers, repeated request IDs, and changes to the current load version. A test suite can establish that a specific implementation follows its designed rules for those inputs. It cannot establish real-world accuracy or legal compliance across all lanes and parties.

A reviewer should be able to reconstruct why the agent did or did not book a load without listening to an entire call. The Proof pack for one attempt should include:

Keep the pack minimal and access-controlled. A transcript may contain phone numbers, names, commercial rates, or other sensitive data. Store only what is needed to prove the transaction, apply the organization’s retention policy, and restrict access by role. Redact or tokenize personal information in test data. Do not put raw recordings into a broadly accessible model prompt or general analytics export.

Evidence should be linked to events, not copied into a mutable summary alone. A single structured event log can record acceptance captured, policy passed, commit requested, commit reconciled, and confirmation sent, each with a correlation ID. The audit view should make ordering visible so a reviewer can see whether the system spoke before or after the write.

A booking agent’s dashboard should separate conversational quality from transaction quality. Useful operational counters include: offers captured, offers rejected by policy, human escalations, stale-quote refusals, commit attempts, confirmed commits, ambiguous timeouts, reconciliations, duplicate-write prevention events, amendments, cancellations, and confirmation-delivery failures.

These counters are not performance claims. They are definitions for instrumentation. Before collecting results, define the denominator and time window. For example, “booking completion rate” could mean committed bookings divided by all eligible accepted offers, but only if “eligible,” “accepted,” and “committed” are consistently defined. Exclude test traffic, abandoned calls, and duplicate retries according to a documented rule. Report rejected offers alongside successful commits; otherwise, a system can look more effective by quietly dropping hard cases.

Measure incorrect confirmations as a separately reviewed safety outcome. A transcript classifier can flag possible overstatement, but a human should reconcile the phrase with the TMS event log. Track the state at the moment the phrase was spoken. A metric that only counts whether a booking eventually appeared misses whether the caller was told it was confirmed while the request was still pending.

BCG’s recommendations to make agent decisions transparent and auditable are directionally compatible with this approach, but a consulting article does not define the right local metric or prove savings for a specific brokerage. Select measures with the dispatch, compliance, finance, carrier-relations, and engineering owners who can explain the consequences of an error.

Start in shadow mode. The agent listens or receives a controlled test transcript, retrieves candidate data, and proposes a decision without calling a commit endpoint. Review whether it finds the correct load, uses current terms, classifies exceptions, and produces a complete proof pack. Record errors by field and by cause. Do not turn a clean demo into permission to book.

Next, allow a human to approve a prepared transaction. The system should display the exact terms and policy outcome, identify missing facts, and require a deliberate approval action. The person should not be asked to approve a vague summary. Capture the approver and policy version with the transaction.

Only then consider limited autonomy, starting with a narrow set of repeatable loads and known carriers where the organization can define the policy and handle exceptions. Exclude freight categories the business does not understand or cannot verify from its data. Put rate limits, per-load locks, expiry, rollback or cancellation procedures, and kill switches in place. Have a dispatcher monitor live outcomes and a separate compliance owner review sampled evidence.

Expansion should follow demonstrated local performance, not a vendor’s general automation percentage. A reported percentage for another workflow, organization, or use case is not transferable. Before increasing autonomy, review failures, near misses, reversals, disputes, human override rates, and the time needed to reconcile ambiguous transactions. A single high-impact state error may outweigh many successful low-risk calls.

A confident but incomplete read-back. The agent omits an accessorial, stop, equipment condition, or time zone. Fix the accepted-terms schema and require a full tuple hash; do not rely only on a prompt asking the model to be careful.

A stale load after a long conversation. Capacity or appointment details change before commit. Revalidate the current version immediately before the write and treat a version mismatch as a fresh negotiation.

A tool timeout with unknown outcome. A create request may have committed. Query by the same idempotency key and keep the caller in a pending state until reconciliation determines whether a booking exists.

A duplicate booking after retry. The client uses a new request key for the same intended action. Enforce idempotency at the transaction layer and test replay behavior, not just client-side retry code.

Caller or carrier misidentification. A plausible voice or caller ID is mistaken for verified authority. Require a trusted identity path and escalate changes to payment or carrier identity through a separate process.

A policy ceiling inferred from negotiation. The model treats a previous approved rate as permission to make a new offer. Retrieve a versioned policy for each transaction; never let the model edit the policy.

Misleading confirmation language. The agent says the load is booked before the TMS confirms. Bind language templates to transaction state and test spoken phrases against durable events.

Concurrent negotiation. Two agents make offers against the same load. Lock or reserve by load version, and require an atomic check before commit.

A cancellation races with booking. One system is cancelling while another finalizes. Serialize changes or reconcile event order before telling either party that a final state exists.

Compliance data unavailable. The agent cannot confirm capability or driver constraints. Fail closed and route to the responsible human; do not substitute a general answer from model knowledge.

The test matrix is intentionally small enough for a broker’s team to adapt in a review session. First, replace generic fields with the organization’s actual load schema and authority policy. Do not copy the sample rate rules as if they were business terms. Second, assign one owner to each assertion: operations for load identity and appointments, carrier compliance for eligibility, finance or pricing for rate authority, and engineering for transaction integrity.

For each case, write three artifacts before running it: a fixture, an expected decision, and an expected spoken status. The fixture should set every relevant value explicitly, including timestamps, policy versions, and current load version. The expected decision says whether the agent may request a commit. The expected spoken status states what the caller should hear if the result is pending, blocked, committed, or escalated.

Then inject the failure at a specific boundary. In the timeout case, simulate “server committed, response lost” and “server never received request” separately. In the stale-quote case, change the quote time between acceptance and commit. In the concurrent-agent case, allow two agents to read the same version and ensure only one can reserve it. Assertions should check the durable final record, not just the response returned to the model.

A test run should store its versioned inputs and outputs in the same proof format as a real transaction, while clearly marking the record as synthetic. Reviewers should be able to reproduce the expected decision with the policy snapshot and fixture. If the result depends on undocumented model behavior or a human’s memory, the test is not reproducible enough for a release gate.

This guide was prepared on 10 November 2026. The original asset is a designed matrix of 14 synthetic acceptance scenarios plus an illustrative evaluator. The sample size is 14 scenario definitions, not 14 transactions observed, and no statistical estimate is derived from them. No live call, customer, carrier, booking, rate, TMS, or vendor was tested. The code example was reviewed as a design illustration but was not executed against production systems.

Topic selection used a targeted Search Console snapshot for 1–8 November 2026 on an existing logistics article. The query “voice ai for logistics booking and rate negotiation” showed 59 impressions, zero clicks, and average position 8.6; “ai call booking for shipping companies” showed 27 impressions, zero clicks, and average position 9.4. These are site-specific observations over one short period, not market demand estimates, representative search behavior, or a prediction of ranking. Queries and average positions can shift with time, device, geography, and aggregation.

Research review included official transportation guidance, an official commodity-flow survey, an open-access review of transportation AI, a consulting perspective on supply-chain agents, a trade-publication product interview and video summary, and one public social discussion. The interviewee’s claims remain attributed to that interview; the social discussion remains anecdotal. A video transcript summary was available on the publication page; no independent audio measurement was made. Search-result coverage is a snapshot and cannot establish that every existing article or workflow omits the same control.

The recommendation may not fit every operator. A small brokerage with a narrow and stable carrier network may need fewer system boundaries than a multinational with several TMS platforms. A low-value, reversible reservation has a different risk profile from hazardous, temperature-controlled, high-value, or contract-sensitive freight. The design should scale to the consequence and the governing contracts, not be copied mechanically.

Voice AI can be useful for gathering offers, negotiating within a controlled range, and preparing a booking. The decision to let it commit should depend on whether the organization can prove the state transition—not on whether the agent sounds certain.

Run the 14-case matrix against a synthetic or sandbox implementation. Require exact terms, explicit authority, a fresh load version, idempotent writes, reconciliation after timeouts, and truthful spoken statuses. If any case permits the model to improvise missing authority or to announce a booking before a durable record exists, keep the workflow in human-approved mode.

The reliable outcome is not “the AI said yes.” It is a versioned, auditable record that matches the terms accepted by an authorized party, plus a confirmation whose wording reflects that record.

Roger Ebert Called Disney's Attempt To Update A Classic Cartoon Character 'Transcendently Bad'

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Kids of today likely won't have heard of the visually-impaired cartoon millionaire known as Mr. Magoo. But for a large part of the 20th Century he was a pop culture fixture who entertained generations of kids with his slapstick shenanigans. By 1997, however, his appeal had waned, which makes Disney's decision to reboot the IP with a Leslie Nielsen-led live-action film all the more confounding. Thankfully, Roger Ebert was on-hand to let the studio have it — which he dutifully did with a scathing review that, bizarrely enough, actually awarded the film half a star.

The critic handed out some damning reviews in his time. Ebert hated Leonard Nimoy's box office flop "Funny About Love" and even walked out of the Oscar-winning war movie "Mediterraneo." All of which left filmmakers terrified of being handed the dreaded "thumbs down" for their efforts. Around 60 movies earned this mark of shame during the critic's career but somehow, "Mr. Magoo" managed to eke out half a star. If you read Ebert's thoughts on the film you'll no doubt wonder how it managed such a feat.

In his review, Ebert revealed that the only thing he liked about the film was the titular character's red Studebaker — that was it. Otherwise, he considered "Mr. Magoo" to be so bad it "soars above ordinary badness as the eagle outreaches the fly." How, then, did this ill-advised comedy reboot manage to get a half-star out of the old boy? Did that Studebaker really save "Mr. Magoo" from the thumbs down club? It's hard to imagine given just how much Ebert hated this movie.

There are plenty of movies Roger Ebert hated that are worth watching, but "Mr. Magoo" isn't one of them. The character first appeared in the 1949 theatrical short "The Ragtime Bear," where he was voiced by none other than future "Gilligan's Island" star and legendary character actor Jim Backus. Backus voiced the United Productions of America (UPA) character until 1977, portraying him in theatrical cartoons, TV series, and TV specials. While Mr. Magoo's character developed over the course of his existence, he's best known as a lovably oblivious old millionaire, whose extreme nearsightedness sees him wander into dangerous situations only to miraculously escape unharmed — causing significant distress to his nephew and frequent sidekick Waldo.

While Backus stopped voicing the character after the 1977 TV series "What's New, Mr. Magoo?" the visually-impaired millionaire endured. The most recent incarnation of the character appeared in the "Mr. Magoo" cartoon, which wrapped up in 2023. Prior to that, however, Disney decided that kids of the mid-'90s needed to be reintroduced to Quincy Magoo, and hired Hong Kong filmmaker Stanley Tong to make it happen.

In what turned out to be Tong's one and only English-language film, the frequent Jackie Chan collaborator teamed up not with a martial arts legend but with Leslie Nielsen. The "Naked Gun" star played the eponymous millionaire in a film that saw him hunted by the FBI and criminals eager to recover a priceless gem to which Mr. Magoo is entirely oblivious. Much of the movie follows the tried-and-true formula of its title character getting himself into absurd situations and coming out unscathed, such as when he commandeers an ironing board to ski after thieves. The movie made $28.9 million on a $30 million budget and upset Roger Ebert considerably.

In his half-star review of "Mr. Magoo," Roger Ebert described the film as being "transcendently bad," and accused it of being entirely without laughs. The critic admitted to never having been a fan of the original cartoons, which he recalled as essentially telling the same joke over and over. Likewise, the film was, in Ebert's estimation, "a one-joke movie without the joke."

Interestingly enough, this might be the only time he ever took aim at the bloopers, which play during the film's credits and evidently failed to amuse the critic. "Even the outtakes at the end aren't funny," he wrote, "and I'm not sure I understood one of them, unless it was meant to show stunt people hilariously almost being drowned." Ebert wasn't even impressed with Leslie Nielsen, who'd long since established himself as a comedic force but was, according to the reviewer, "unable to overcome the fact that a little Magoo at six minutes in a cartoon is a far different matter than a lot of Magoo at 90 minutes in a feature."

On "Siskel & Ebert," neither of the hosts could bring themselves to say anything nice about the movie. Gene Siskel called it "uninspired" before Ebert took the opportunity to once again lay into the movie, calling it a "wet blanket." Roger Ebert was wrong about a fair few movies in his time, but a 10% critic score on Rotten Tomatoes suggests he was right on the money with this one. And yet, he gave the movie half a star. Why? Did the Studebaker really save it from the shame of a thumbs down rating?

→ Baca artikel asli

Indiana Jones Star John Rhys-Davies Slams The Dial Of Destiny

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

James Mangold's 2023 adventure film "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny" was met with a lot of ambivalence back when it was first released, and star John Rhys-Davies, who has played the role of Sallah in two of the previous movies, wasn't fond of the way it turned out either. In a video interview posted on the Off/Frame Instagram account, Rhys-Davies admitted to being very disappointed in the film, largely because it focused too much on all-new characters, rather than on Indy (Harrison Ford), and himself:

"I'll be honest with you, I was very disappointed in it. The film is meant to be a nostalgia film. They filled the cast up with people we had never met before, so there can be no nostalgia, really. I found the scenes that I'd been promised in the thing were cut immediately after I signed the contract. I think it's a disappointing film."

"Dial of Destiny," despite catching up with Indiana Jones in the year 1969, and featuring Sallah in a small role, was more focused on the adventures of the irascible Helena Shaw (Phoebe Waller-Bridge), Indy's adult goddaughter and something of a con woman who sells artifacts on the black market. Helena is a great character, I'd say, but Rhys-Davies has a point in that the movie was a little too focused on her, and not on the nostalgic characters the audience paid money to see.

And as an actor, of course, he has every right to be upset about his part being reduced after he signed his contract to appear in the movie. Rhys-Davies doesn't go into detail about what larger role Sallah was to play in "Dial of Destiny," but in the finished film he certainly doesn't play a major role at all.

John Rhys-Davies also noted that Antonio Banderas appears in "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny," and that he wasn't utilized correctly either. Banderas plays a frogman named Renaldo whose screentime is very brief indeed. Regarding Banderas, Rhys-Davies said:

"And what is tragic, really, is ... I mean, there are a number of tragic things. I mean, Banderas, the most exciting actor of his generation. [...] I think he's a wonderful actor. But, really, what a terrible thing to do to him.

Some might defend the tone and the visuals of "Dial of Destiny," which are largely accurate to Steven Spielberg's Indy movies, but no one could argue that Antonio Banderas or John Rhys-Davies were used well. Perhaps the filmmakers didn't want to write a 2023 adventure story with two actors — Ford and Rhys-Davies — who were born in the 1940s.

It certainly could have been cheaper. "Dial of Destiny" was, according to some reports, a $419 million movie, making it possibly one of the most expensive movies of all time. This means that its worldwide box office total of just under $384 million didn't come close to turning a profit. /Film even saw the writing on the wall a week before the movie's release: "Dial of Destiny" wasn't going to make nearly enough.

Now, had the filmmakers scaled back on the film's action mayhem and caught up with an elderly Indiana Jones and Sallah who spent their time in libraries and slowly trekking into quiet caves, maybe you could have had something. Perhaps John Rhys-Davies could have played a much more central role. But then, an action-light Indiana Jones movie might be anathema to the nature of the character.

It has only been a few years since the release of "Indiana Jones and the Dial of Destiny," and the film doesn't seem to have undergone any kind of major relitigation in that time. The weary ambivalence towards the legacy sequel still seems to be firmly in place.

Reviews of "Dial of Destiny" were generally good, but no critic seemed to be all that overwhelmed. /Film's review, by Lex Briscuso, was positive, giving the film an eight out of 10, and said that "the film has a fun plot that feels completely true to the sense of adventure the series has always brought to its audience."

Although Owen Gleiberman's review in Variety just as accurately called it "a dutifully eager but ultimately rather joyless piece of nostalgic hokum." "Dial of Destiny" was the first Indiana Jones movie not to be directed by Steven Spielberg, and the entire affair felt like a tired victory lap with many new figures juggling the baton. It didn't help that star Harrison Ford was almost 81 when the movie was released to the public. He was hardly the same action star he was in "Raiders of the Lost Ark," released 42 years prior, and wasn't as well-suited for the fighting and whip-swinging the character was better known for.

"Dial of Destiny" isn't a low-quality embarrassment, but it's also simultaneously too large and too small. It only ever felt like a means to milk more money out of 1980s I.P., and never a serious step in the evolution of the character. John Rhys-Davies wasn't the only one who was disappointed.

Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

US President Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the chief executive of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

Trump said he spoke to Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia, which has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

The company is the world's second most valuable company after Apple and designs computer chips that have become central to AI technology. The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off Nvidia's value on Monday as its share price fell by more than 4%.

Trump later told reporters at the White House that the FBI was now looking into the issue, but did not elaborate on the probe or when it was launched. The FBI declined to comment when contacted by the BBC.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia, the company at the centre at the development of AI worldwide through its microchips. She is not related to the Nvidia chief.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Nvidia's Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

The American firm, co-founded by Huang, is also a major focus in the AI rivalry between the US and China, with officials in Washington treating the company's exports to Beijing as a matter of national security.

Huang and Nvidia were also sued on Monday in a proposed class action lawsuit in California accusing them of defrauding shareholders by concealing the chief executive's ties with a suspected Chinese spy.

The lawsuit alleges that the firm lied to the media about Huang's relationship with Alice Huang, "who is suspected of seeking to help smuggle Nvidia products to China."

The BBC has contacted Nvidia for comment on the lawsuit.

At the White House, Trump was asked multiple times, including by the BBC, when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

Trump said he thought Huang was an "honorable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president said.

He said he spoke to Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing, and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Nvidia designs advanced computer chips that power AI models and is at the centre of the US and China's technology race - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked national security fears.

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Huang has admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

In the letter, the chairmen of the committees said they were seeking a briefing "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

It is unclear whether Huang will appear. Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Some workers the BBC spoke to at Nvidia's Santa Clara headquarters on Monday told the BBC that it was business as usual, despite the intense attention on the company.

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI buildout that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Nvidia's most advanced AI semiconductors including the GB200 Blackwell super chip remain blocked from the Chinese market.

Scotty’s Greatest Star Trek Quote That Fans Love Didn’t Happen In The Original Series

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Star Trek fans love what's possibly Scotty's (James Doohan) most memorable quote, but he said it in Star Trek: The Next Generation, not Star Trek: The Original Series. Captain Scott's reappearance in Star Trek: The Next Generation season 6, episode 4, "Relics," gave us one of Scotty's most fun quotes that many fans also agree with.

The beloved Scottish Chief Engineer of the USS Enterprise NCC-1701 and 1701-A appeared in the original Star Trek, Star Trek: The Animated Series, and seven Star Trek movies. The younger Lieutenant Montgomery Scott's (Martin Quinn) exploits are chronicled in Star Trek: Strange New Worlds. Through it all, Scotty is a wellspring of quotes that are fun to mimic in the engineer's Scottish accent.

Scotty complaining, "I canna' change the laws of physics!" or "I'm giving her all she's got!" when Captain James T. Kirk (William Shatner) demands the impossible from the "miracle worker" are iconic. Scotty saying, "Hello, computer" to a 1986 Apple computer's mouse in Star Trek IV: The Voyage Home never ceases to be funny. Also unforgettable: "Captain, there be whales here!"

However, Scotty discovering the USS Enterprise-D's holodeck in "Relics" delivered an all-timer quote.

Perhaps Scotty's greatest quote that Star Trek fans love to repeat is in Star Trek: The Next Generation's "Relics." Drunk on green Aldebaran whiskey, Scotty found the holodeck and requested to see the bridge of "the Enterprise." When the computer asked to specify between the five existing USS Enterprises on record, the irritated Scotty spelled out what he wanted:

It's worth wagering that for the 34 years since "Relics" premiered, Star Trek fans at home have said, "No bloody A, B, C, or D," along with Scotty when rewatching that moment. Everyone sympathizes with Scotty, who is loyal to the original Starship Enterprise he served aboard. Fans may love other versions of the Enterprise, especially the Galaxy-class USS Enterprise-D, but we all know why Scotty loves his Enterprise.

At the time that Star Trek: The Next Generation was in production, there were only five starships named USS Enterprise, and Scotty named them all. Scotty was Chief Engineer of the original Constitution-class Enterprise and its replacement, the Enterprise-A. He also stepped aboard the Excelsior-class USS Enterprise-B and the USS Enterprise-D. Scotty only missed the doomed Enterprise-C.

"Relics" was also a remarkable achievement in production and set design, perfectly recreating the 1960s USS Enterprise bridge on its 1990s TV budget. The older Captain Scott visiting his original bridge was a lightning bolt of nostalgia, and perhaps the most powerful evocation of the original Star Trek by The Next Generation. It was a simpler time and a simpler ship that Scotty yearned for.

Newly arrived in the 24th century in Star Trek: The Next Generation's "Relics," it's understandable that Scotty was taken aback by five USS Enterprises having existed. Captain Scott may have been even more dismayed by how many more Enterprises joined the fleet, including a retcon of Star Trek's past.

Patrick Stewart announcing his comeback as Jean-Luc Picard 8 years ago left fans overjoyed, and let him make history that Star Trek may not repeat.

Enterprise, the first Star Trek prequel series, retconned the NX-01 as the first Starship Enterprise. Commanded by Captain Jonathan Archer (Scott Bakula), NX-01 was Starfleet's first Warp 5 starship, and it was the Enterprise that pioneered space exploration, forging the alliances that led to the United Federation of Planets. After a refit, NX-01 was renamed USS Enterprise and is now at the Fleet Museum on Athan Prime.

A couple of years after Scotty left the USS Enterprise-D, the Galaxy-class Federation flagship was destroyed in Star Trek Generations. It was replaced by the Sovereign-class USS Enterprise-E, commanded by Captain Picard and then, Captain Worf (Michael Dorn). The E was mysteriously "lost" and succeeded by the Odyssey-class USS Enterprise-F.

Star Trek: Picard season 3 ended with the USS Titan-A renamed the USS Enterprise-G, commanded by Captain Seven of Nine (Jeri Ryan). Enterprise's Temporal Cold War showed the Universe-class USS Enterprise-J, and Star Trek: Discovery established that the 32nd century Starfleet has an unseen USS Enterprise.

That's six more Starship Enterprises created after Star Trek: The Next Generation for Scotty to frown on.

Star Trek: The Next Generation is streaming on Paramount+

Select Water (WTTR) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Select Water Solutions, Inc. (WTTR -6.86%) reported record operational performance for the first nine months of the year, driven by significant volume growth in its infrastructure segment. Management stated that the recent acquisition of Pilot Water expands the company's disposal capacity and complements its existing recycling network in the Delaware Basin. The company indicated that the divestment of a majority stake in Peak Rentals was a strategic move to concentrate resources on core water management and chemical technologies. Management noted that new contract expansions and acreage dedications are expected to provide a strong growth profile for contracted earnings heading into 2027.

Underpinning this performance, we grew produced water volumes to 1.7 MMbpd during the third quarter of 2026. This is a 14% increase relative to the second quarter of 2026 and a significant year-over-year increase of more than 30% relative to the third quarter of 2025.

These volume gains drove Water Infrastructure segment revenues up by more than 40% year-over-year to a record level of $110 million for the third quarter of 2026. With ongoing strength in our base Water Infrastructure business, we anticipate a strong finish to the year for this segment. Furthermore, as recently announced earlier this month, we closed on our strategic acquisition of Pilot Water Solutions. We are already underway with post-closing customer outreach and integration planning, and I am increasingly excited about what this transaction will do for the company long term. With new contracted projects coming online from both Select's base business and the acquired Pilot Water business in 2027, and significant remaining capacity available for commercial expansion, we are well-positioned for a strong growth profile in the years ahead.

With the continued execution of our organic growth backlog and the high volume of inbound calls from our customers post-acquisition, we have increased conviction around the commercialization opportunities of the combined network. We continue to win additional projects that underwrite our network and volume expansion, and I believe 2027 will be an active year for new commercialization activities. We expect the combination of our complementary recycle and disposal assets will deliver superior end-to-end water management that reduces costs for our customers and allows Select to maximize the value out of every barrel that comes through our network. Elsewhere, our Water Services and Chemical Technologies segments also generated strong performance relative to guided expectation for the third quarter.

On the Water Services side, we completed our efforts to streamline this segment with a successful recapitalization and divestment of the majority interest in our Peak Rentals business late in the third quarter. This transaction further concentrates our business around our core water solutions and Chemical Technologies thesis, and we are pleased to retain long-term exposure to Peak Rentals' growth potential through our minority ownership position in the business. Looking ahead, we anticipate current customer activity levels to support solid results in our Water Services and Chemical Technologies segments, while acknowledging standard near-term seasonality in the fourth quarter ahead.

In our Water Infrastructure segment, we expect to see benefits from our strong secular tailwinds, ongoing organic project development, and a growing opportunity set for the combined Select and Pilot Water networks. I am proud of our year-to-date performance, which has set the stage for another record year for the company. While the broader macro and geopolitical environment continues to evolve, we remain confident in the company's future, and I look forward to another record year ahead for Select in 2027 with the full-year benefit of the Pilot Water acquisition. In summary, our growth initiatives and optimization efforts, combined with our strong balance sheet and contracted earnings profile, position Select to deliver continued value creation for our shareholders.

At this point, I will hand it over to Chris to speak to our financial results and our outlook in a bit more detail. Chris?

Chris George: Thank you, John, and good morning, everyone. Select made great strides in the third quarter of 2026, which included consolidated revenue, gross margin, net income, and Adjusted EBITDA growth relative to the second quarter, record Water Infrastructure revenue, and another quarter of record Adjusted EBITDA. Additionally, subsequent to quarter end, Select completed a debut senior unsecured notes offering, raising $900 million of long-term eight-year financing at 7.5%, 8%. The offering, which was very well received and successfully upsized by $100 million from initial launch, was primarily used to fund the acquisition of Pilot Water, as well as to refinance Select's prior sustainability-linked credit facility and term loan.

Additionally, in connection with the closing of the Pilot Water acquisition, Select also closed on a new five-year, $500 million cash flow revolving credit facility. This new revolving credit facility remains undrawn as of today and nearly doubles Select's available liquidity as compared to our prior credit facility. These efforts position Select with a strong balance sheet and one of the leading credit ratings and leverage profiles amongst our water midstream peer group, including a leverage ratio of approximately 2x net debt to Adjusted EBITDA on a pro forma basis following the close of the Pilot Water acquisition.

Of note, this is before taking into account the expected growth we forecast for 2027 or the benefit of synergies from the Pilot Water integration. Looking at our third quarter consolidated financial performance in more detail, we grew revenue to $412 million, generating net income of $23 million and Adjusted EBITDA of more than $93 million. The Water Infrastructure segment delivered another solid quarter marked by top-line growth and the execution of key strategic objectives. We grew produced water volumes handled in the third quarter to 1.7 MMbpd, driving record revenues of $110 million and relatively steady 57% gross margins before D&A.

This represents an 8% increase in revenue and a 6% increase in gross profit before D&A as compared to the second quarter of 2026. Importantly, though, this equates to year-over-year growth in revenue and gross profit before D&A of about 40% and 50%, respectively, relative to Q3 2025. Looking ahead to the fourth quarter, the Water Infrastructure segment will benefit from a partial quarter contribution from the Pilot Water business, resulting in anticipated revenue growth of 35%-45%, or about $150 million-$160 million for the segment. On a combined basis, we expect gross margins in the 52%-56% range during Q4, as certain integration-related costs and seasonal expenses impact the margins of the segment during the quarter.

With our strong year-to-date performance and forecasted sequential growth in the base business, we are well-positioned to deliver above the high end of our previously increased 2026 guidance of 25%-30% year-over-year growth, before taking into consideration any benefit of the Pilot Water acquisition. As John mentioned, we expect the Pilot Water business to finish full year 2026 within the previously outlined guidance range of $250 million-$270 million of revenue and $100 million-$110 million of Adjusted EBITDA. Similarly, the business remains on track to achieve the 2027 outlook of $120 million-$130 million of Adjusted EBITDA, driven primarily by new MVCs and other capital projects coming online in early 2027.

Additionally, we continue to set the stage for further organic Water Infrastructure growth in the base business in 2027, with additional contract wins. Most notable was our previously announced Northern Delaware Basin contract expansion we signed during August with a key investment-grade customer, which added more than 250,000 new acres under dedication and converted more than 100,000 acres from ROFR to base dedication, while also extending the term another 12 years with increased rate on an existing contract. Switching over to Water Services, this segment saw revenues grow by approximately 7% quarter-over-quarter, outpacing our guidance of flat revenue sequentially, driven by continued strength in the Permian Basin.

Gross margins before D&A in Water Services increased to 24% during Q3, an improvement compared to 23% in the second quarter. Notably, as John referenced, from a strategic standpoint, we completed the recapitalization and divestment of the majority interest in Peak Rentals during late September. The transaction resulted in the sale of a 55% majority interest in the business, with Select receiving $77 million of consideration, subject to customary fees and post-closing adjustments, including $42 million of cash proceeds and a $35 million seller note, while retaining a 45% minority ownership interest in the business.

While we benefited from Peak's contribution to the income statement for effectively the full third quarter, with the transaction closing in late September, we ultimately deconsolidated Peak Rentals' financials from our balance sheet at quarter end, resulting in an equity method investment retained on the balance sheet. Going forward, this divestment and deconsolidation will reduce Water Services' financial results, with Peak having represented about 21% of the segment's revenue year to date, while generating relatively comparable gross margins to the overall segment.

Additionally, concurrent with the transaction closing, Peak Rentals entered into new long-term financing agreements on a non-recourse basis to Select, providing the Peak business with its own capital structure and liquidity moving forward, allowing the business to independently fund its own future growth capital while allowing Select to benefit from Peak's future growth through its retained equity interests. Looking ahead to the fourth quarter specifically, we anticipate revenue of $150 million-$160 million for Water Services, driven primarily by the impact of the Peak divestiture, along with standard fourth quarter seasonality. We also forecast margins before D&A in the 21%-24% range for the quarter.

Comparing the year-to-date performance with our fourth quarter guidance, even with the absence of Peak in Q4, we are well on track to surpass our original full year 2026 guidance for the segment. Overall, we believe Water Services remains well-positioned to protect its leading market share position and improve its margin profile over time. Elsewhere, the Chemical Technologies segment generated revenue of $90 million during the third quarter, coming in on the high end of our $85 million-$90 million guidance, as we pulled forward certain customer activity into the third quarter, which, along with standard Q4 seasonality, is expected to result in $75 million-$80 million of revenue in Q4.

Gross margins before D&A are expected to remain relatively steady in the 17%-18% range during Q4 relative to the 18% result in the third quarter. While the fourth quarter will likely see a modest pullback in the Chemical Technologies segment, this segment has performed very well year to date, and with full year 2026 revenue expected to increase approximately 10%-12% on a year-over-year basis, we should finish the year well above our original 2026 guidance for this segment. We remain optimistic about the outlook for this business, as our new product development efforts continue to see great results and strong interest with the customer base.

On a consolidated basis in the third quarter, SG&A increased to $54 million, or approximately 13% of revenue, attributable primarily to increased transaction-related costs associated with the Peak Rentals and Pilot Water transactions. Looking ahead, we anticipate SG&A of 11%-12% of revenue in the fourth quarter of 2026 before returning to more normalized levels of approximately 10%-11% of revenue in 2027. Looking back on a consolidated basis, supported by revenue gains and strong gross margins, altogether, we generated consolidated Adjusted EBITDA of $93.5 million during the third quarter of 2026, on the high end of our guidance range of $90 million-$94 million, resulting from healthy performance across all three segments.

Looking forward, we anticipate Adjusted EBITDA of $95 million-$100 million for the fourth quarter of 2026, as continued strong performance in the Water Infrastructure business and a partial quarter contribution from the Pilot Water business more than offset the full quarter impact from the sale of Peak Rentals and fourth quarter seasonality. Additionally, we remain poised for meaningful year-over-year growth in 2027, as incremental Pilot MVC volumes and previously announced projects already under development for Select drive continued contracted expansion of our Water Infrastructure business in the year ahead. With Pilot now closed and consolidated budget efforts well underway, I look forward to providing more specific details on the full year 2027 outlook on our year-end call.

Looking at our other costs for the near term, D&A expense should climb slightly in the fourth quarter to $50 million-$55 million after giving effect to both the Peak and Pilot transactions during the quarter, though this remains subject to preliminary purchase price accounting estimates and allocations. Net interest expense increased sequentially to $8 million and is expected to increase further to $15 million-$17 million in the fourth quarter, driven by Select's recently closed senior notes offering, which was completed subsequent to quarter end. From a cash flow perspective, we saw relatively steady operating cash flow of $84 million in the third quarter relative to $87 million in the second quarter.

On the investing side, we deployed $96 million in the third quarter towards a combination of CapEx, acquisitions, and deconsolidation adjustments associated with the Peak transaction. Net CapEx during Q3 was $88 million, with bolt-on acquisitions totaling another $2 million, primarily supporting projects in our Water Infrastructure business. With the addition of Pilot Water, our full-year maintenance capital program on a go-forward basis is expected to increase to approximately $80 million-$90 million, though still very representative of a maintenance-like business model given the overall earnings power of the business.

We do anticipate some incremental spend in the fourth quarter of 2026 associated with Pilot Water's ongoing capital projects, as well as a modest amount of initial CapEx associated with upgrading and integrating the networks. Accordingly, for 2026, we now anticipate net capital expenditures to modestly increase to $300 million-$310 million, attributable primarily to the Pilot Water acquisition. Overall, I am very pleased with the strong year-over-year trajectory across all parts of the business so far in 2026.

We have made significant progress in strengthening Select as a company this year, including scaling our Water Infrastructure platform through both investment and acquisition, increasing our liquidity position with our latest financings, and enhancing the contractual weighting of our earnings with a sizable and growing MVC and acreage dedication contract portfolio. With that, I will hand it over to the operator for any questions. Operator?

Immunovant (IMVT) Q2 2027 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Immunovant drops Sjögren's and points to 2027 readouts.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Immunovant, Inc. (IMVT +15.13%) reported that its lead candidate, IMVT-1402, met the primary endpoint in a Phase 2 trial for difficult-to-treat rheumatoid arthritis, with the 600 mg dose achieving a 92.5% ACR20 response at week 28. Management indicated a strategic decision to deprioritize further development in Sjögren’s disease, citing unfavorable clinical data from competitors and the evolving market landscape. Parent company Roivant Sciences reported a commercial launch for LISRAYA, yielding $31.0 million in initial revenue and broad early physician adoption. The company confirmed it is on track for major data readouts in Graves’ disease and myasthenia gravis in 2027 while maintaining a balance sheet intended to fund operations through its first potential commercial launch.

I want to jump now to another update that I wasn't totally expecting to provide today, but it's a great update, which is that Period 2 of the IMVT-1402 RA study has now read out. As a reminder on Slide 9 of the way the study worked, this was a randomized withdrawal trial in which in Period 1, all patients were on 600 mg, that's the high dose of 1402, and we reported that data. As a reminder, we had 70%+ ACR20 responses, 50%+, kind of mid-50s ACR50 responses, and around 35% ACR70 responses at the end of that Period 1.

In Period 2, those patients who had achieved an ACR20 response were then put into a randomized withdrawal portion, where they were either kept on 600 mg or randomized down to 300 mg or placebo. Then the primary endpoint was ACR20 at the end of that 12-week period, which you can sort of think of as an ACR20 maintenance endpoint, in that it's 100% of those patients roughly started or were randomized into this, had ACR20 responses. So if you look at Slide 10, it was 118 patients had that ACR20 response, and 117 of them were effectively re-randomized for the efficacy analysis in Period 2, or 118 were re-randomized, 117 were analyzed.

The disposition was pretty straightforward in terms of balance across the arms. As a reminder, before we get to the actual data on slide 11, this is a big unmet medical need, right? These patients have all failed multiple lines of therapy. Many of them have failed TNFs and JAK inhibitors. Some of them have also failed IL-6s, et cetera. We think there's about 85,000 patients in the U.S.

We'll talk more about the commercial opportunity here at our upcoming Investor Day in December, but this is a large number of patients who have struggled to get adequately treated, and we know that autoantibody pathology matters, at least in some of these patients, and it's increasingly clear that we have found a subset of patients for whom it matters quite a lot. These patients on slide 12 have significant morbidity and quality of life impairment. A significant portion of them are fully work disabled for over a decade. They have many consultations with their physicians. A large number of them are dependent on external help to live. A lot of them require surgery.

A lot of them cannot walk or report walking disabilities. These are really struggling patients with late-line RA. This enrolled -- the trial on 13 enrolled a difficult-to-treat population. These were fourth line plus patients having failed conventional therapy, and again, 2-plus mechanisms of advanced therapy. You can see a little bit about this. This is all from the previously reported data on Slide 13. The arms on Slide 14 were reasonably well-balanced once you got into the randomized withdrawal period. Similar disease severity, similar balance of prior meds, et cetera. A pretty well-balanced study. I think what we saw on Slide 15 was just phenomenal across a couple of dimensions.

Remember, 100% of these patients approximately had an ACR20 response at the beginning of this period. Almost all of them, 92.5%, still had an ACR20 response at the end of 28 weeks, whereas only 75%-ish had an ACR20 response on placebo. Notably, that got a p-value for the 600 mg arm. Notably, and I think this is an important finding, 300 mg was not enough to preserve the response.

300 mg looked pretty similar to placebo, which I think is just a further reinforcement of something we've believed all along, which is that depth of IgG suppression really matters across FcRn indications, and that it took a higher dose with deeper IgG suppression to deliver the sustained clinical benefit in this patient population. You can see on slide 16, similarly great data on ACR50 and ACR70. You start to run into N issues when you get to ACR70, but we hit a p-value on ACR50 as well. 80% of patients on 600 mg had, I think, a nominal p-value, the stats weren't worked, 80% on ACR50 had an ACR50 response at that week 28 versus 56% on placebo.

Again, nice separation without a nominal p-value on ACR70 at week 28. Consistent data across depth of response. I really like slide 17, in that you can just see over time that these patients are just, it looks like they're significantly benefiting.

This is the cohort of patients who were on 600 mg for the full period, and you can see already some meaningful ACR50 and ACR70 responses as early as week 4, and those responses really continue to get better and are well-preserved through the end of the study, even though after week 16, there starts to be a, "placebo effect", where some of these patients were where the patients thought they might have been taken off drug at that point. This data looks great, and in my opinion, supports a clinically meaningful profile for these patients that is pretty exciting.

You can see on slide 18 just a little bit more data here on separation from placebo on CDAI and SDAI, which reflect widely used scales in clinical practice for assessing sustained response. You can see these placebo patients really worsened, whereas the drug patients were able to preserve their response through week 28, which is pretty exciting. The other thing I'll just add on slide 19 is looking at the JAK experience subset. The JAK experience subset did just about as well as the overall population. Three-quarters of them, 75% on placebo, have maintained an ACR20 response. So, there appears to be some sort of durability of clinical benefit, which is interesting. I think against the backdrop of 75% of these ACR20 responders still had a response at the end, I think the fact that we were able to preserve north of 90% of those responders on drug is pretty exciting, again, in a patient population that has really struggled across others.

So, I think we're pretty excited about both the sort of durability of off-drug effect as well as the durability of continuing on-drug effect and the fact that we saw a continued build in some of the responder rates, especially at ACR50 and ACR70 over time. I think it is just pretty interesting data overall, and we think that translates to an exciting commercial opportunity. Again, I am looking forward to --one of the things we are going to do on our Investor Day is spend some time digging in on this and why we are so excited about it. I will plant that as a flag for please come and listen. I think that will be a good session.

I think the data really did us a solid here.

Operator: The next question is coming from Ash Verma with UBS.

Ashwani Verma: Just on 1402 for difficult-to-treat RA. You mentioned the FDA interaction is the next step. Can you talk a little bit about the initial thoughts on what you think would be the right clinical trial design here? Is it likely a regimen that would be a combo with methotrexate or TNFs, or are you thinking of monotherapy? Just in terms of trial design, is it likely to be parallel or randomized withdrawal?

Matthew Gline: Look, I think the first thing is, the right study design here is the one that gets the drug approved, so that's ultimately got to be a conversation together with FDA. We think this population has a big effect. A lot of the particulars in terms of endpoint and so on are going to be discussions. I think it's relatively unlikely this will be a randomized withdrawal study. I think it's pretty likely, just given the division and the way other RA studies have worked, this will be a straightforward RCT.

I think other than that, in terms of patient population, it will be quite similar to what we've done here in the phase II-B in this study, because it's a good patient population. We expect a lot of collaboration from FDA, and we think we'll get to a path forward here. Again, I think the questions are end and specifics around endpoints and things like that. I think we're hoping for a reasonable dialogue with FDA on those points. There's a pretty broad range of potential outcomes depending on how FDA approaches the questions, and we're looking forward to sharing that once we've got it.

Operator: I am showing no further questions at this time. I will now turn the call back over to Matthew Gline for closing remarks.

Matthew Gline: Look, it's been a really fun year for us. I think there's no substitute in this business for generating clinical data that matters, and we've had a lot of opportunity to do that across multiple indications. It's tremendously rewarding when something like the LISRAYA approval and launch happens, where you plan for something for a long time, you work really hard as a team on getting it there, you generate data that matters for patients, FDA agrees with you, then you get to actually see patients on drug using it, physicians excited to make it a part of their practice. It's a really great moment, and it feels amazing.

Huge kudos to the Roivant team, to the Priovant team, to the Pulmovant team, the Immunovant team. These things are a ton of work, and even in the AI age, there is no substitute for hard work and pavement pounding, and it has been a lot of that. Then, again, just thank you to the patients in our studies who have made this possible, the investigators, and now to the patients and the physicians who are entrusting us with their care in dermatomyositis. It is a great moment for Roivant, and I am looking forward to lots of good stuff coming.

Thank you, everybody, and looking forward to seeing many of you in just a few short weeks here for our Investor Day. Have a great day.

Operator: This concludes today's conference call. Thank you for participating, and you may now disconnect.
